\noindent Each argument below is set as its Lean source sets it: the module docstrings as prose, in the order they are written, and under each one the package, line of reason, countermodel or result it introduces. Atoms are numbered propositional variables, numbered once per argument, so a variable means the same claim in every position within it; the legend gives each one's claim, its classification, the tradition that holds it and the source that carries it.

\noindent Nothing here asserts that the premises are true --- only that the conclusion does or does not follow from them.

\section*{Born in Bethlehem — Micah 5:2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Born in Bethlehem — Micah 5:2}

\subsection*{Arguments.BornInBethlehem — Phase 1 vertical slice}
Matthew 2:5–6 quotes Micah 5:2 as grounds that the Messiah is born in Bethlehem. Both the Christian predictive reading and a critical reading are encoded, and the argument is carried through to a \texttt{Satisfaction\allowbreak{}Witness} — the first end-to-end connection from cited premises to a claim that a person meets a messianic criterion.

\texttt{jesus\allowbreak{}Born\allowbreak{}In\allowbreak{}Bethlehem} is cited to scripture alone. That is visible in the generated manifest via \texttt{scripture\allowbreak{}Only\allowbreak{}Atoms}, and it should be: the historicity of the Bethlehem birth is disputed in critical scholarship, and an argument that assumes it on the authority of the texts whose reading is in question is assuming part of what it sets out to show.

The argument is short enough to stay in one file. It runs on a \textbf{single line of reason}, which is exactly why it is the weakest of the fulfilment arguments and why \texttt{critical\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}establishes} costs the rival so little: deny the one interpretive premise and nothing is left standing. Compare \texttt{Born\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}AVirgin}, which runs on four.

\subsection*{The claims, numbered}

 & claim & kind & held & source \\

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{quotation\allowbreak{}Edge}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Matthew explicitly quotes Micah — uncontroversial verbal citation, attested across traditions.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def quotationEdge : IntertextEdge :=
  { fromPassage := Scripture.matthew2_6, toPassage := Scripture.micah5_2,
    relation := RelationType.quotation,
    source := Scripture.na28Apparatus Scripture.matthew2_6 }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{predictive\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The Christian predictive reading of Micah 5:2.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def predictiveReading : Interpretation :=
  { passage := Scripture.micah5_2,
    reading := "Micah 5:2 predicts the Messiah's birthplace as Bethlehem",
    asRelation := some RelationType.prediction,
    source :=
      {
        primary :=
          Reference.work Bib.keilDelitzschMinorProphets
            (Bib.Locus.adLoc Scripture.micah5_2),
        tradition := Tradition.christianTypological,
        confidence := Confidence.wellSupported } }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{born\allowbreak{}In\allowbreak{}Bethlehem}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The criterion this argument establishes a candidate must meet.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def bornInBethlehem : FulfillmentCriterion :=
  { name := "born in Bethlehem",
    basis := predictiveReading }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{cite}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Citation and classification for every atom. Total, so nothing is uncited.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def cite : Claim → AtomMeta
\end{verbatim}}
\paragraph*{The line of reason}
\leavevmode\par
One strand, and the argument's whole weight is on it.

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{to\allowbreak{}Criterion}}\par\nobreak\noindent
From the predictive reading and Matthew's intent, the criterion follows.

\[(P_{1} \land P_{3}) \rightarrow P_{6}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{to\allowbreak{}Fulfilment}}\par\nobreak\noindent
From the criterion and the historical claim, the fulfilment follows.

\[(P_{6} \land P_{4}) \rightarrow P_{7}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{predictive\allowbreak{}Line}} --- Predictive reading of Micah 5:2\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The predictive line.} Micah's oracle read as prophecy, Matthew's quotation read as a fulfilment claim, and the criterion that follows.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{1} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{2} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{3} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{3}) \rightarrow P_{6} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{6}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{critical\allowbreak{}Line}} --- Critical reading of Micah 5:2 as a near-term oracle\par\nobreak\noindent
The critical line: the quotation is granted and the prediction denied, so the same step delivers nothing.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{2} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{5} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & \lnot P_{1} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{3}) \rightarrow P_{6} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{6}
\end{align*}
\paragraph*{Packages}
\leavevmode\par
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{christian}} --- Christian predictive reading of Micah 5:2\par\nobreak\noindent
The Christian predictive argument.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{1} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{2} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{3} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{4} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{3}) \rightarrow P_{6} \\
  \text{(6)} \quad & (P_{6} \land P_{4}) \rightarrow P_{7} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{7}
\end{align*}
\emph{Grounded in scripture alone:} Jesus of Nazareth was born in Bethlehem.
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{critical}} --- Critical reading of Micah 5:2 as a near-term oracle\par\nobreak\noindent
The critical reading: the quotation is granted, the prediction is not.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{2} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{5} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & \lnot P_{1} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{3}) \rightarrow P_{6} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & (P_{6} \land P_{4}) \rightarrow P_{7} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{7}
\end{align*}
\emph{Grounded in scripture alone:} Jesus of Nazareth was born in Bethlehem.
\paragraph*{Results}
\leavevmode\par
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{christian\_\allowbreak{}establishes}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Given the Christian premises, the conclusion follows.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem christian_establishes : Establishes christian
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{critical\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The critical reading, written down: Micah's oracle concerns a contemporary Judaean ruler, so no messianic birthplace criterion arises and nothing about Jesus follows from it.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def criticalReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.micahPredictsBethlehem => False
    | Claim.messiahBornInBethlehem => False
    | Claim.jesusSatisfiesCriterion => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{critical\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}establishes}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The critical reading does not establish the conclusion.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem critical_not_establishes : ¬Establishes critical
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{jesus\_\allowbreak{}satisfies\_\allowbreak{}bethlehem}}\par\nobreak\noindent
End to end: under the Christian package, Jesus satisfies the Bethlehem criterion. This is the first result in the library that runs from cited premises through a machine-checked entailment to a claim about a person.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem jesus_satisfies_bethlehem : Satisfies jesus bornInBethlehem
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\paragraph*{Satisfiability}
\leavevmode\par
\texttt{Entails} is vacuously true over a premise set with no model, so a package built from contradictory premises would establish its conclusion with every gate passing. Only the package carrying a positive \texttt{Establishes} result needs checking: \texttt{critical} has a countermodel already, and a countermodel is a valuation satisfying every premise.

Not tagged \texttt{@[headline]} — a claim about the health of the encoding rather than about Micah.

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{everything\allowbreak{}Holds\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The reading on which every claim in this argument holds at once.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def everythingHoldsReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{christian\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The Christian package has a model, so \texttt{christian\_\allowbreak{}establishes} is not vacuous.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem christian_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable christian.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}

\section*{Born of a virgin — Isaiah 7:14, Genesis 3:15, Micah 5:2–3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Born of a virgin — Isaiah 7:14, Genesis 3:15, Micah 5:2–3}

\subsection*{Arguments.BornOfAVirgin — Isaiah 7:14, Genesis 3:15, Micah 5:2–3}
Matthew 1:22–23 quotes Isaiah 7:14 as grounds that the Messiah is born of a virgin. Unlike Micah 5:2 (see \texttt{Born\allowbreak{}In\allowbreak{}Bethlehem}), the predictive reading here is heavily disputed, and the dispute is lexical: the Hebrew עַלְמָה (\emph{almah}) means "young woman" and does not by itself mean "virgin", while the Septuagint's παρθένος does. Isaiah's immediate context — a sign to Ahaz, apparently fulfilled within the prophet's own generation (cf. Isa 8:3–4) — is read by most critical and much Jewish scholarship as having nothing to do with a future Messiah.

The argument now runs on \textbf{four scriptural strands}, and that changes its shape. Each is encoded as a \texttt{Line} — its own grounds, its own inference step, what it delivers — and the packages are assembled from those rather than from flat premise lists. \texttt{Lines.lean} is where the shape of the argument is legible in one screen.

\textbf{The Isaianic strand} is the one above, hinging on \emph{almah}.

\textbf{The protoevangelium strand} reads Genesis 3:15 — enmity between the serpent's seed and "her seed", זֶרַע הָאִשָּׁה — as the first promise of a redeemer, and takes the matrilineal wording to mark a birth with no human father, since Hebrew ordinarily reckons זֶרַע through the man. That last step is genuinely contestable and is marked \texttt{disputed}: Genesis itself speaks of a woman's seed in wholly ordinary contexts (Gen 16:10 to Hagar, 24:60 to Rebekah), so the wording at 3:15 is not the anomaly the argument needs it to be unless the reader already grants the messianic reading.

\textbf{The Michean strand} reads Micah 5:2–3 — "until the time when she who is in labour has given birth" — as naming a mother and no father for the coming ruler. The objection is immediate and is encoded as the rival: this is an argument from silence, and an oracle about a woman in labour has no occasion to mention a father.

\textbf{The referential strand answers the lexical dispute without entering it.} \texttt{almah} need not \emph{mean} "virgin" for Mary to \emph{be} an \texttt{almah}. Genesis settles the compatibility outright: Rebekah is an עַלְמָה at 24:43 and a בְּתוּלָה "whom no man had known" at 24:16, the same woman under both terms. Mary was a betrothed young woman of marriageable age and conceived as a virgin, so she answers Isaiah's description on anyone's lexicon — and \texttt{semantic\_\allowbreak{}establishes} shows that Matthew's claim therefore never required the sense the objection demands.

That reply has a price, and \texttt{compatibility\_\allowbreak{}does\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}establish\_\allowbreak{}criterion} names it: the argument is purely defensive. It defeats the objection without delivering the criterion, which still has to come from one of the four scriptural strands. What it buys is that \emph{losing} the lexical dispute no longer costs the fulfilment claim.

\textbf{The versional evidence is encoded in full, including the half that tells against the reading.} Targum Jonathan renders עוּלֵימְתָא, "young woman", and does not read 7:14 messianically though it reads 9:5–6 and 11:1 so; Aquila, Symmachus and Theodotion render νεᾶνις. Against them, the pre-Christian Septuagint renders παρθένος and the Syriac Peshitta ܒܬܘܠܬܐ, both "virgin" — so the Aramaic evidence is itself split, Jewish against Christian. 1QIsaᵃ confirms עַלְמָה in the Hebrew, so no textual variant rescues either side; the dispute is purely semantic. The usual Christian reply, that the Three revised against Christian use, is recorded but not leaned on: Theodotion's version is arguably pre-Christian, and the module says so rather than passing over it.

\texttt{compatibility\_\allowbreak{}defeats\_\allowbreak{}lexical\_\allowbreak{}objection} is where the tension pays off. Granting every versional datum and adding the referential premises, the objection no longer reaches its conclusion — because once virginity is compatible with the word, four translators making four different choices about how much of the referent to make explicit is not evidence about the sense.

\textbf{Wegner's objection is encoded in its sharpest form, and the module shows the circle in it rather than alleging one.} Wegner does not merely say that עַלְמָה means "young woman". He says that הָרָה at Isaiah 7:14 is a predicate adjective, so the clause reads "the עַלְמָה \emph{is} pregnant" — already, when the sign is given — and a pregnant woman is not a virgin; the one clear Isaianic case then settles the word. \texttt{wegner\_\allowbreak{}establishes} grants him every premise and checks that the argument goes through, because a rival worth answering has to be stated at full strength first.

The parse is not the weak point, and the encoding says so by granting it throughout: Rydelnik — cited here through Postell, who reports him at 474 — reads the same predicate adjective and draws the opposite conclusion, that the virgin \emph{is} pregnant and the sign is therefore as deep as Sheol. What the grammar does not supply is that the pregnancy is an \emph{ordinary} one, and that is the premise that excludes virginity. \texttt{wegner\_\allowbreak{}needs\_\allowbreak{}ordinary\_\allowbreak{}pregnancy} removes it and the objection stops. \texttt{reading\allowbreak{}Supplies\allowbreak{}Ordinary\allowbreak{}Pregnancy} then supplies it the way Wegner's own case does — from the near-term reading of the sign — and with the lexical conclusion turned back against that reading, the two legs close. \texttt{circle\_\allowbreak{}leaves\_\allowbreak{}the\_\allowbreak{}lexical\_\allowbreak{}conclusion\_\allowbreak{}open} and \texttt{circle\_\allowbreak{}leaves\_\allowbreak{}the\_\allowbreak{}denial\_\allowbreak{}open} are the result: keep every uncontested datum, and \emph{neither} end is settled — not established, and not refuted either. A cycle of implications is satisfied outright by a valuation on which all of its nodes are false, and equally by one granting Wegner's own conclusion. Nothing enters the loop, so nothing comes out of it, in either direction. That is a demonstration rather than a complaint, and it is conditional in the usual way: a defender of Wegner should deny \texttt{reading\allowbreak{}Supplies\allowbreak{}Ordinary\allowbreak{}Pregnancy} and argue that the near-term reading stands on the historical evidence alone.

Postell's second reply is independent of the first and survives that defence. The other clear עַלְמָה passages Wegner himself lists are cases of virgins, so a single referent cannot be what settles the denotation — the same principle would settle it the other way. \texttt{usage\_\allowbreak{}parity\_\allowbreak{}blocks\_\allowbreak{}wegner} checks it, and the atom is marked \texttt{plausible} rather than \texttt{well\allowbreak{}Supported} because Postell's "in fact virgins" is stronger than Wegner's own discussion of Song 6:8.

\textbf{The oldest reply goes to the ordinary pregnancy itself.} Justin, Irenaeus and Origen answer the near-term reading with one argument. The oracle promised a \emph{sign}, offered "in the depth or in the height" (7:11), and an ordinary conception happens to every woman who is not barren, so it would be no sign. Rydelnik makes the same move today. \texttt{sign\allowbreak{}Line} encodes it, and it denies the one ground of Wegner's objection that the grammar does not supply. \texttt{sign\_\allowbreak{}leaves\_\allowbreak{}the\_\allowbreak{}lexical\_\allowbreak{}conclusion\_\allowbreak{}open} measures what that buys: with the fathers' grounds in place of the ordinary pregnancy, Wegner's lexical conclusion is independent of what remains. The objection is blocked, and nothing is shown about the word.

The rival is written first, and Wegner holds it himself: a sign need not be a miracle. Isaiah's own children are "signs and portents" (8:18), and this sign is dated by a child's infancy (7:16), so its point may be its timing. Rhodea argues the same. \texttt{reply\_\allowbreak{}leaves\_\allowbreak{}the\_\allowbreak{}pregnancy\_\allowbreak{}open} shows that the reply blocks the fathers in turn, without supplying Wegner's ordinary pregnancy.

\texttt{Dispute.Wegner} puts the three positions together. Every one of them has a \texttt{disputed} weakest link, so the dispute ties (\texttt{nothing\_\allowbreak{}prevails\_\allowbreak{}over\_\allowbreak{}wegner}). Both the fathers alone and Wegner with the reply are defensible resolutions of it. What would decide it is one of two ratings: whether the sign of 7:14 must be the kind offered at 7:11, and whether the reply's inference survives Rydelnik, who grants its grounds and denies its conclusion.

The critical case against the Isaianic strand is now \emph{derived} rather than assumed. \texttt{critical} used to carry \texttt{¬isaiah\allowbreak{}Predicts\allowbreak{}Virgin\allowbreak{}Birth} as a bare premise; it now carries the step that produces it — that a sign given for Ahaz's generation is not also a prediction of a virgin conception — and \texttt{critical\allowbreak{}Denial\_\allowbreak{}establishes} checks that the step works. Berry supplies the objection: how Isaiah 7:14 was fulfilled in Ahaz's own day is itself an open question, and \texttt{berry\_\allowbreak{}blocks\_\allowbreak{}critical\_\allowbreak{}denial} shows that granting him that much takes the exclusion out. It does no more than that. It does not establish the predictive reading and it does not touch the lexical dispute.

Berry also supplies a \emph{defensive} lexical premise — that the semantic range of עַלְמָה does not exclude "virgin" — and \texttt{admissibility\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}enough} measures what it is worth: nothing, on its own. \emph{May mean} is not \emph{does mean}, and the whole Isaianic strand lives in that gap.

\textbf{The compositional strand} (Postell) declines the lexical fight altogether. Isaiah 7 sits inside Isaiah 2–12, a unit framed by eschatological vision at 2:1–4 and 11:1–12:6; on the principle that an oracle's meaning in a finished book is set by its literary placement rather than by the events it describes — argued from the making of canonical Jeremiah — 7:14 anticipates a future, miraculous birth. The hinge is \texttt{composition\allowbreak{}Governs\allowbreak{}Meaning}, and a reader who holds that an oracle's historical setting fixes its sense rejects it outright.

Postell also supplies the module's sharpest rival-defeater. \texttt{parity\allowbreak{}Defeats} \texttt{Near\allowbreak{}Term\allowbreak{}Exclusion} observes that Isaiah 9:5–6 and 11:1–10 are read as messianic without reservation \emph{and} stand on the same near-term Assyrian timeline as 7:14 — the invasion of 8:7–8, the oppressor's rod of 10:5. So a near-term setting cannot be what rules out messianic reference, or it would rule out 9 and 11 too. \texttt{parity\_\allowbreak{}blocks\_\allowbreak{}critical\_\allowbreak{}denial} checks it. Notably the Targum is cited \emph{for} this premise: it reads 9:5–6 and 11:1 messianically while declining to read 7:14 so, which makes it a hostile witness and therefore a strong one.

That gives the critical exclusion step two independent defeaters. Berry's is evidential — we do not know how the sign was fulfilled in Ahaz's day. Postell's is structural — the near-term setting was never the right kind of reason.

\textbf{Taken together, the positions make a dispute, and the dispute has an outcome the entailment results cannot state.} \texttt{Dispute.lean} treats the scriptural reading, the critical denial, Berry, Postell's two counterexamples and Motyer as the nodes of an argumentation framework and proves who defeats whom — a defeat being an attack derived from entailment that the cited confidences do not block. Against the scriptural reading alone, \texttt{nothing\_\allowbreak{}prevails\_\allowbreak{}unanswered}: the critical denial and the scriptural reading defeat each other, because the weakest premise on each side is \texttt{disputed} (\texttt{christian\_\allowbreak{}defeats\_\allowbreak{}critical}). Hear Berry and Postell, and \texttt{scriptural\_\allowbreak{}reading\_\allowbreak{}prevails\_\allowbreak{}once\_\allowbreak{}replies\_\allowbreak{}are\_\allowbreak{}heard}: nothing defeats Postell's counterexamples, they defeat the critic, and so they defend the scriptural reading — and Berry, and Motyer with Compton — against the only party that defeats them. The critic is left with no defence at all (\texttt{critical\_\allowbreak{}denial\_\allowbreak{}indefensible}). Inference steps are rated like premises, and Berry's and Motyer's are \texttt{disputed}, so alone they tie with the critic: \texttt{nothing\_\allowbreak{}prevails\_\allowbreak{}on\_\allowbreak{}motyer\_\allowbreak{}alone}, and \texttt{motyer\_\allowbreak{}rests\_\allowbreak{}on\_\allowbreak{}his\_\allowbreak{}inference} shows the weight of his reply is on that step. The outcome turns on Postell's inference being \texttt{plausible} — no cited source grants his grounds and keeps the exclusion. He gives two counterexamples, from Isaiah 9 and 11 and from Micah 5; either suffices (\texttt{scriptural\_\allowbreak{}reading\_\allowbreak{}prevails\_\allowbreak{}without\_\allowbreak{}postell}), and without both nothing prevails (\texttt{nothing\_\allowbreak{}prevails\_\allowbreak{}without\_\allowbreak{}the\_\allowbreak{}counterexamples}). It turns too on the critic's premises being \texttt{disputed}, as the replies, Motyer and Compton contest them.

\textbf{Luke is a second witness to the conception, not to the prophecy.} His account is parallel to Matthew's and independent of it, but it never quotes Isaiah 7:14. Luke 1:31 is close to the Septuagint's wording, and whether that is an allusion is disputed: \texttt{luke\allowbreak{}Allusion\allowbreak{}Edge} records it as one, attributed, and \texttt{annunciation\allowbreak{}Form\allowbreak{}Edge} records the rival reading, on which the wording is the stock birth-announcement form that Isaiah 7:14 itself uses. Luke adds to \texttt{independent\allowbreak{}Attestation}; he adds nothing to the rating of the predictive reading.

\texttt{hinges\_\allowbreak{}jointly\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing} is the result worth having. No single interpretive hinge carries the argument any more — strip \emph{almah} and the other three strands still deliver the criterion, which \texttt{almah\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing} records. Strip all four and the argument collapses. \texttt{almah\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing\_\allowbreak{}alone} keeps the pre-Miravalle finding on the books: within the Isaianic strand taken by itself, the lexical premise still carries everything.

\textbf{The magisterial strand is encoded separately and is not part of \texttt{christian}.} Papal teaching on Mary's virginity in conceiving, as collated by Miravalle, is a Roman Catholic premise; this library's author does not grant that magisterial teaching settles doctrinal questions, and the encoding says so rather than quietly folding it in. \texttt{catholic} is the package for a reader who does grant it. \texttt{magisterial\_\allowbreak{}authority\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing} shows what follows for a reader who does not: that route yields nothing on its own, and the four scriptural strands have to carry the argument by themselves. The rival is cited to Westminster I.x.

Matthew's and Luke's birth narratives agree on the virgin conception while differing in nearly every other detail, and that convergence is encoded as a multiple-attestation premise — though the independence of the two traditions is itself debated, and is marked \texttt{plausible} rather than \texttt{well\allowbreak{}Supported}.

\subsubsection*{Where things are}
This argument is a directory, because at fourteen hundred lines it was one file that no one could hold in view. The dependencies run one way. Where a file outgrew three hundred lines of code, the material for the dispute over Wegner's objection, and the citations for the dispute over the critical denial, went into modules of their own beside it.

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.45\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.45\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{File} & \textbf{Contents} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\texttt{Atoms.lean} & the \texttt{Claim} atoms, the recurring sources, the intertextual edges, the criterion \\
\texttt{Sources.lean} & \texttt{cite}: a citation and a classification for every atom \\
\texttt{Sources/Near\allowbreak{}Term.lean} & the citations of the dispute over the critical denial \\
\texttt{Sources/Wegner.lean} & the citations of the dispute over Wegner's objection \\
\texttt{Lines.lean} & the inference steps, and the lines of reason they compose into \\
\texttt{Packages.lean} & the positions, and the variant packages the results refute \\
\texttt{Results.lean} & the \texttt{@[headline]} results about the strands, with their trust base \\
\texttt{Results/Wegner.lean} & the results about Wegner's objection, its circle, and the sign \\
\texttt{Dispute.lean} & the critical denial, the scriptural reading and four replies as one dispute \\
\texttt{Dispute/Wegner.lean} & Wegner, the fathers' sign argument and the near-term reply as another \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
Shared material lives further out: passages and citation bundles in \texttt{Testimony.Scripture}, the \texttt{Line} and \texttt{case\allowbreak{}Of} vocabulary in \texttt{Testimony.Logic.Line}, and the \texttt{establish} and \texttt{refute\_\allowbreak{}with} tactics in \texttt{Testimony.Logic.Tactic}.

\subsection*{Arguments.BornOfAVirgin.Atoms — the atomic claims, and what cites them}
The atoms this argument is built from, the \texttt{Source} shapes that recur across them, the typed intertextual edges between the passages, and the criterion the argument is about. Everything here is \emph{what the argument is about}; nothing here asserts anything.

See \texttt{Testimony.Arguments.Born\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}AVirgin} for the dispute this encodes.

\paragraph*{Recurring sources}
\leavevmode\par
Five works carry this argument, and each carries several atoms at the same pinpoint with the same confidence. Written out at every atom, the citation was repeated rather than the claim — and a \texttt{Source} differing from its neighbour by one field is hard to read as deliberate.

These are values, so an atom that cites one of them \emph{with something further} says so with \texttt{\{ ... with supporting := ... \}}, and the difference is the part a reader sees.

\subsection*{The claims, numbered}

$P_{25}$ & Matthew 1:23 quotes Isaiah 7:14 & textual & critical scholarship, consensus & \cite[app. Matt 1:23]{na28-2012}; \cite{ubs5-2014} \\

$P_{28}$ & An oracle's meaning in the finished book is set by its literary placement & interpretive & Christian, hist.-gramm., disputed & \cite[483--486]{postell-isaiah-messianic-2025} \\

$P_{30}$ & Isaiah 9 and 11 sit on the same near-term Assyrian timeline as 7:14 & textual & Christian, hist.-gramm., well supported & \cite[487--489]{postell-isaiah-messianic-2025}; Isa 8:7-8; Isa 9:8-11; Isa 10:5 \\

\noindent\footnotesize \textsuperscript{\dag} grounded in scripture alone: the reading is assumed, not argued for.
\normalsize

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{motyer\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Isaiah}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Motyer on Isaiah 7:14: the predictive reading and the lexical premise it needs, disputed and marked so.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def motyerOnIsaiah : Source :=
  {
    primary :=
      Reference.work Bib.motyerIsaiah (Bib.Locus.adLoc Scripture.isaiah7_14),
    tradition := Tradition.christianTypological,
    confidence := Confidence.disputed }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{miravalle\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Mary}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Miravalle's \emph{Meet Your Mother} at pages 9–10.

Eight atoms and three intertextual edges cite exactly this, which is a fact about the argument worth being able to see: \textbf{both} the protoevangelium and Michean strands rest on one source, in one tradition, at one pinpoint. Every one of them is \texttt{disputed}.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def miravalleOnMary : Source :=
  { primary := Reference.work Bib.miravalleMeetMary (Bib.Locus.pages 9 10),
    tradition := Tradition.romanCatholic, confidence := Confidence.disputed }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{brown\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Birth}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Brown's \emph{Birth of the Messiah}, taken whole: the critical case.

\texttt{confidence} defaults to \texttt{well\allowbreak{}Supported}, which is what the critical readings are. It is passed explicitly where a critical premise is contested: the one Brown's case \emph{presupposes} rather than argues, and both premises of the critical denial, each denied by scholars this argument cites.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def brownOnBirth : optParam Confidence Confidence.wellSupported → Source :=
  fun confidence =>
    { primary := Reference.work Bib.brownBirthMessiah,
      tradition := Tradition.criticalScholarship, confidence := confidence }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{postell\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Isaiah}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Postell on the compositional reading, at a given pinpoint. The pinpoint matters here in a way it does not for the others: the textual observations and the interpretive principle come from different pages and carry different confidence.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def postellOnIsaiah : Bib.Locus → Confidence → Source :=
  fun l confidence =>
    { primary := Reference.work Bib.postellIsaiahMessianic l,
      tradition := Tradition.christianHistoricalGrammatical,
      confidence := confidence }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{wegner\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Almah}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Wegner on the sense of עַלְמָה, at a given pinpoint.

Like \texttt{postell\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Isaiah}, the pinpoint carries weight: the grammatical claim is at 471 and the near-term reconstruction that makes its conclusion bite is at 476–478, and they are not equally secure. Wegner is an evangelical arguing \emph{against} a virgin-birth prediction here, which is what makes him the module's strongest lexical rival rather than one more critical voice.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def wegnerOnAlmah : Bib.Locus → Confidence → Source :=
  fun l confidence =>
    { primary := Reference.work Bib.wegnerVirginBirths l,
      tradition := Tradition.christianHistoricalGrammatical,
      confidence := confidence }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{france\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Matthew}}\par\nobreak\noindent
France on Matthew 1:23, for what Matthew's quotation intends and what follows from it.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def franceOnMatthew : optParam Confidence Confidence.wellSupported → Source :=
  fun confidence =>
    {
      primary :=
        Reference.work Bib.franceMatthew
          (Bib.Locus.adLoc Scripture.matthew1_23),
      tradition := Tradition.christianHistoricalGrammatical,
      confidence := confidence }
\end{verbatim}}
\paragraph*{Intertextual edges and readings}
\leavevmode\par
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{quotation\allowbreak{}Edge}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Matthew explicitly quotes Isaiah, via the Septuagint's παρθένος.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def quotationEdge : IntertextEdge :=
  { fromPassage := Scripture.matthew1_23, toPassage := Scripture.isaiah7_14,
    relation := RelationType.quotation,
    source := Scripture.na28Apparatus Scripture.matthew1_23 }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{protoevangelium\allowbreak{}Edge}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Genesis 3:15 read as promise rather than quotation. Typed as \texttt{.promise}, not \texttt{.prediction}: what Miravalle argues is that the protoevangelium \emph{commits} to a redeemer born of a woman, and collapsing that into a prediction would beg the question the Michean and Isaianic strands are also asking.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def protoevangeliumEdge : IntertextEdge :=
  { fromPassage := Scripture.matthew1_23, toPassage := Scripture.genesis3_15,
    relation := RelationType.promise,
    source := miravalleOnMary }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{micah\allowbreak{}Maternal\allowbreak{}Edge}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Micah 5:3's maternal reference, read as a messianic theme rather than a verbal link — there is no quotation of Micah 5:3 in the birth narratives.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def micahMaternalEdge : IntertextEdge :=
  { fromPassage := Scripture.matthew1_23, toPassage := Scripture.micah5_3,
    relation := RelationType.messianicTheme,
    source := miravalleOnMary }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{luke\allowbreak{}Allusion\allowbreak{}Edge}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{Luke's annunciation, read as an allusion to Isaiah 7:14.} Luke never quotes the verse, but Luke 1:31 is close to its Septuagint wording, and Luke 1:27 and 1:32 bring in the house and the throne of David, as Isaiah 7:13 and 9:6–7 do. Whether that is an allusion is disputed, and both sides are recorded here: Davies and Allison find an influence from Isaiah 7:14, Fitzmyer rejects it, as Rhodea reports (71 n. 66). Typed \texttt{.allusion}, never \texttt{.quotation}: that is the difference between Luke and Matthew. See \texttt{annunciation\allowbreak{}Form\allowbreak{}Edge} for the rival reading of the same wording.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def lukeAllusionEdge : IntertextEdge :=
  { fromPassage := Scripture.luke1_31, toPassage := Scripture.isaiah7_14,
    relation := RelationType.allusion,
    source :=
      {
        primary :=
          Reference.work Bib.rhodeaDidMatthewConceive (Bib.Locus.page 71),
        supporting :=
          [Reference.scripture
              [Scripture.verseIn TextualTradition.nestleAland28
                  Scripture.luke1_31,
                Scripture.verseIn TextualTradition.septuagint
                  Scripture.isaiah7_14]],
        tradition := Tradition.christianHistoricalGrammatical,
        confidence := Confidence.disputed } }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{annunciation\allowbreak{}Form\allowbreak{}Edge}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The rival reading: the wording is the birth-announcement form.} Luke 1:31 is as close to the announcement to Hagar (Gen 16:11) as to Isaiah 7:14, and Isaiah 7:14 is itself an instance of the form, which Brown and others trace through Ishmael, Isaac, Samson, John and Jesus (Johnson 270 n. 6; Young 113–14). On this reading the shared wording is shared form, not dependence — typed \texttt{.thematic}.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def annunciationFormEdge : IntertextEdge :=
  { fromPassage := Scripture.luke1_31, toPassage := Scripture.genesis16_11,
    relation := RelationType.thematic,
    source :=
      {
        primary :=
          Reference.work Bib.johnsonSamsonTypeScene (Bib.Locus.page 270),
        supporting :=
          [Reference.work Bib.youngImmanuelProphecy (Bib.Locus.pages 113 114),
            Reference.work Bib.brownProblemVirginalConception
              (Bib.Locus.page 31)],
        tradition := Tradition.criticalScholarship,
        confidence := Confidence.wellSupported } }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{predictive\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The Christian predictive reading of Isaiah 7:14. Far more contested than Micah 5:2 — see the module doc.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def predictiveReading : Interpretation :=
  { passage := Scripture.isaiah7_14,
    reading :=
      "Isaiah 7:14 predicts a virgin conceiving and bearing 'Immanuel', " ++
        "fulfilled in the virgin birth of the Messiah",
    asRelation := some RelationType.prediction,
    source := motyerOnIsaiah }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{protoevangelium\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Miravalle's reading of Genesis 3:15: the seed is named as the woman's, and no man is involved in the conception it promises.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def protoevangeliumReading : Interpretation :=
  { passage := Scripture.genesis3_15,
    reading :=
      "Genesis 3:15 promises a redeemer born of the woman's seed, with no " ++
        "human father implied by the wording",
    asRelation := some RelationType.promise,
    source := miravalleOnMary }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{maternal\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Miravalle's reading of Micah 5:2–3: the oracle names a mother and no father for the ruler who comes forth from Bethlehem.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def maternalReading : Interpretation :=
  { passage := Scripture.micah5_3,
    reading :=
      "Micah 5:2–3 names only the woman in labour as bearing the coming " ++
        "ruler, mentioning no father",
    asRelation := some RelationType.messianicTheme,
    source := miravalleOnMary }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{born\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}AVirgin}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The criterion this argument establishes a candidate must meet.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def bornOfAVirgin : FulfillmentCriterion :=
  { name := "born of a virgin",
    basis := predictiveReading }
\end{verbatim}}
\subsection*{Arguments.BornOfAVirgin.Sources.NearTerm — the citations of the near-term dispute}
The atoms of the dispute over the critical denial, cited here and dispatched from \texttt{cite} by name: the critic's two premises, that 7:14 was a near-term sign to Ahaz and that such a sign excludes a messianic sense, and the grounds of the replies to them — Berry's, Postell's Micah counterexample, and Motyer's with Compton's. (Postell's Isaiah counterexample rests on atoms the compositional strand also uses, and is cited with them in \texttt{Sources.lean}.)

They sit apart for the reason \texttt{Sources.Wegner} does: \texttt{cite} stays one total function, and each value below is reached from exactly one of its cases.

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{isaiah\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Near\allowbreak{}Term\allowbreak{}Sign\allowbreak{}To\allowbreak{}Ahaz\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{isaiah\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Near\allowbreak{}Term\allowbreak{}Sign\allowbreak{}To\allowbreak{}Ahaz}. The reading Justin already answers, in Trypho's form: fulfilled in Hezekiah (Dial. 67, 77). Origen's reply is a question — which child of Ahaz's day was called Immanuel? — and his conclusion that the sign was given to the house of David (Cels. I.35). Motyer denies it most directly (1970, 120, 124): the sign confirms events after the fact, and Maher-shalal-hash-baz, not Immanuel, carries the timetable. So does Compton (2007, 12): 7:14 is addressed to the house of David, in plural pronouns, and only 7:15–16, with a singular "you", to Ahaz. \texttt{disputed} for that reason, on the definition that re-rated the exclusion premise.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def isaiahIsNearTermSignToAhazCited : AtomMeta :=
  {
    label :=
      "Isaiah 7:14 is a near-term sign to Ahaz, fulfilled in Isaiah's generation",
    kind := PremiseKind.interpretive,
    source := brownOnBirth Confidence.disputed }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{near\allowbreak{}Term\allowbreak{}Excludes\allowbreak{}Messianic\allowbreak{}Sense\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{near\allowbreak{}Term\allowbreak{}Excludes\allowbreak{}Messianic\allowbreak{}Sense}. The premise Berry and Postell deny, and Motyer with them: \texttt{disputed} by the library's own definition, since the contest is recorded in \texttt{berry\allowbreak{}Line} and \texttt{postell\allowbreak{}Line}. It was once cited \texttt{well\allowbreak{}Supported} on Brown's authority, which let the critic outrank both replies. Compton (2007, 12–14) denies it on a third ground: the near-term part of the oracle, 7:15–16, uses the child's infancy only as a measure of time, and so does not need the child born in Ahaz's day.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def nearTermExcludesMessianicSenseCited : AtomMeta :=
  {
    label :=
      "A sign given for Ahaz's generation is not also a prediction of a virgin birth",
    kind := PremiseKind.interpretive,
    source := brownOnBirth Confidence.disputed }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{near\allowbreak{}Term\allowbreak{}Fulfilment\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Unclear\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{near\allowbreak{}Term\allowbreak{}Fulfilment\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Unclear}. Descriptively uncontroversial: the near-term referent has been taken for Isaiah's son, for Hezekiah, and for a son of Ahaz, with no settled answer. What is contestable is the use Berry puts it to. Compton surveys the candidates (5) and finds that none fits (9).

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def nearTermFulfilmentIsUnclearCited : AtomMeta :=
  {
    label :=
      "How Isaiah 7:14 was fulfilled in Ahaz's own day is itself an open question",
    kind := PremiseKind.interpretive,
    source :=
      {
        primary :=
          Reference.work Bib.berryVirginBirth (Bib.Locus.pages 1653 1654),
        supporting :=
          [Reference.work Bib.comptonImmanuelProphecy (Bib.Locus.pages 5 9)],
        tradition := Tradition.christianHistoricalGrammatical,
        confidence := Confidence.wellSupported } }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{sign\allowbreak{}Given\allowbreak{}To\allowbreak{}House\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}David\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{sign\allowbreak{}Given\allowbreak{}To\allowbreak{}House\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}David}. What the Hebrew says: שִׁמְעוּ־נָא בֵּית דָּוִד and לָכֶם in 7:13–14 are plural, אַתָּה in 7:16 is singular. Compton builds on it (12), Motyer notes the address to the dynasty (122). The grammar is not in dispute; what it implies is, and that is left to the step that uses it.

{\small\begin{verbatim}

  {
    label :=

    kind := PremiseKind.textual,
    source :=

\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{maher\allowbreak{}Shalal\allowbreak{}Hash\allowbreak{}Baz\allowbreak{}Repeats\allowbreak{}The\allowbreak{}Timetable\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{maher\allowbreak{}Shalal\allowbreak{}Hash\allowbreak{}Baz\allowbreak{}Repeats\allowbreak{}The\allowbreak{}Timetable}. Common ground: the parallel is what leads the critics who identify the two children to identify them (Compton 5, citing Clements). Motyer (124) and Compton (13) read it the other way. The observation is shared; the reading of it is the step.

\texttt{micah\allowbreak{}Ruler\allowbreak{}Faces\allowbreak{}Assyria}. What the text says. Postell cites it by the Hebrew numbering, as Micah 5:1 and 5:4–5 (481 n. 72).

\texttt{micah\allowbreak{}Ruler\allowbreak{}Read\allowbreak{}Messianically}. Granted by the critic's own authority: "there was an expectation of the Messiah's birth at Bethlehem (Mt 2:4-6; Jn 7:42)" (Brown 1972, 26 n. 64). Postell: recognised as messianic by ancient Jewish interpreters (481 n. 72).

\subsection*{Arguments.BornOfAVirgin.Sources.Wegner — the citations of the sign dispute}
The atoms of the dispute over Wegner's objection, cited here and dispatched from \texttt{cite} by name. They are the parse of הָרָה, the pregnancy it reports, what that pregnancy is taken to show, and the fathers' sign argument against it with the near-term reply to that.

They sit apart because \texttt{cite} is one total function over every atom. It keeps that guarantee: each value below is reached from exactly one of its cases, so an atom still cannot go uncited. What moves is only where the citation is written, which keeps \texttt{Sources.lean} short enough to read.

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{harah\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Predicate\allowbreak{}Adjective\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{harah\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Predicate\allowbreak{}Adjective}. Deliberately not the contested step, and granted throughout. Wegner puts it at "most likely" and cites Williams' grammar §75; Rydelnik parses the clause exactly the same way and draws the opposite conclusion from it, that the virgin \emph{is} pregnant and the sign is therefore as deep as Sheol (Isa 7:11).

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def harahIsPredicateAdjectiveCited : AtomMeta :=
  {
    label :=
      "הָרָה at Isaiah 7:14 is a predicate adjective: the עַלְמָה is pregnant",
    kind := PremiseKind.linguistic,
    source :=
      have __src :=
        wegnerOnAlmah (Bib.Locus.page 471)
          Confidence.wellSupported;
      { primary := __src.primary,
        supporting :=
          [Reference.work Bib.postellIsaiahMessianic (Bib.Locus.page 474),
            Reference.work Bib.youngImmanuelProphecy (Bib.Locus.pages 115 116)],
        tradition := __src.tradition, confidence := __src.confidence } }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{isaianic\allowbreak{}Almah\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Already\allowbreak{}Pregnant\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{isaianic\allowbreak{}Almah\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Already\allowbreak{}Pregnant}. Granted on both sides of the dispute over this verse: Wegner reads a present pregnancy and so does Rydelnik. What they disagree about is what kind of pregnancy it is.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def isaianicAlmahIsAlreadyPregnantCited : AtomMeta :=
  {
    label :=
      "The עַלְמָה of Isaiah 7:14 is already pregnant when the sign is given",
    kind := PremiseKind.interpretive,
    source :=
      have __src :=
        wegnerOnAlmah (Bib.Locus.pages 471 472)
          Confidence.wellSupported;
      { primary := __src.primary,
        supporting :=
          [Reference.work Bib.postellIsaiahMessianic (Bib.Locus.page 468),
            Reference.work Bib.youngImmanuelProphecy (Bib.Locus.pages 116 117)],
        tradition := __src.tradition, confidence := __src.confidence } }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{pregnancy\allowbreak{}At\allowbreak{}The\allowbreak{}Sign\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Ordinary\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{pregnancy\allowbreak{}At\allowbreak{}The\allowbreak{}Sign\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Ordinary}. The hinge of Wegner's objection, and not a lexical claim at all. He gets it from the near-term reading of the sign: the child born within nine months, everything in the oracle discharged by 701 BC. Rydelnik, parsing הָרָה as Wegner does, denies it outright. So do the fathers, on the ground that an ordinary conception would be no sign, and \texttt{sign\allowbreak{}Line} is that argument.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def pregnancyAtTheSignIsOrdinaryCited : AtomMeta :=
  { label := "The pregnancy Isaiah 7:14 announces is an ordinary conception",
    kind := PremiseKind.interpretive,
    source :=
      have __src :=
        wegnerOnAlmah (Bib.Locus.pages 476 478)
          Confidence.disputed;
      { primary := __src.primary,
        supporting :=
          [Reference.work Bib.postellIsaiahMessianic (Bib.Locus.page 474)],
        tradition := __src.tradition, confidence := __src.confidence } }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{isaianic\allowbreak{}Almah\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Not\allowbreak{}AVirgin\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{isaianic\allowbreak{}Almah\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Not\allowbreak{}AVirgin}. Wegner's conclusion about the referent. Postell states the step it rests on baldly: "since the עַלְמָה in Isaiah 7:14 is already pregnant, she obviously cannot be a virgin" (468).

{\small\begin{verbatim}

  { label := "The עַלְמָה of Isaiah 7:14 is not a virgin",
    kind := PremiseKind.interpretive,
    source :=

          Confidence.disputed;

        tradition := __src.tradition, confidence := __src.confidence } }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{one\allowbreak{}Referent\allowbreak{}Settles\allowbreak{}Denotation\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{one\allowbreak{}Referent\allowbreak{}Settles\allowbreak{}Denotation}. Wegner's method: Isaiah 7:14 is used as proof that the word cannot mean "virgin". Rico and Gentry deny the principle — "the analysis of the Immanuel oracle does not by itself allow one to draw a conclusion concerning the meaning of the word" — and hold that the other occurrences settle it the other way (quoted at Postell 468 n. 22).

    source :=

        wegnerOnAlmah (Bib.Locus.pages 471 472)
          Confidence.disputed;
      { primary := __src.primary,
        supporting :=
          [Reference.work Bib.postellIsaiahMessianic (Bib.Locus.page 468),
            Reference.work Bib.ricoGentryInfantKing (Bib.Locus.page 152)],
        tradition := __src.tradition, confidence := __src.confidence } }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{other\allowbreak{}Clear\allowbreak{}Almah\allowbreak{}Cases\allowbreak{}Are\allowbreak{}Virgins\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{other\allowbreak{}Clear\allowbreak{}Almah\allowbreak{}Cases\allowbreak{}Are\allowbreak{}Virgins}. Postell at 468 n. 22, of the three passages Wegner calls clearest. Marked \texttt{plausible} rather than \texttt{well\allowbreak{}Supported} because it is stronger than Wegner's own text: Genesis 24:43 is uncontested, but Wegner's discussion of Song 6:8 allows that some of the עֲלָמוֹת in the harem would not be virgins.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def otherClearAlmahCasesAreVirginsCited : AtomMeta
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{sign\allowbreak{}Must\allowbreak{}Be\allowbreak{}Extraordinary\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{sign\allowbreak{}Must\allowbreak{}Be\allowbreak{}Extraordinary}. Origen's form of it: "What kind of sign, then, would that have been — a young woman who was not a virgin giving birth to a child?", tied to the sign offered "in the depth or in the height" (Cels. I.35). Justin (Dial. 84) and Irenaeus (Haer. III.21.6) argue the same. Rydelnik makes the move today, as Postell reports (474). \texttt{disputed}: Wegner grants that the sign offered at 7:11 would have been miraculous, but holds that a sign may be an everyday occurrence and reads the one given at 7:14, after Ahaz refused the first, as one (469–470, 477–478); Rhodea holds that a sign does not require a miracle, and that 7:11 does not make this one (64 and n. 10).

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def signMustBeExtraordinaryCited : AtomMeta
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{ordinary\allowbreak{}Conception\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}No\allowbreak{}Marvel\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{ordinary\allowbreak{}Conception\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}No\allowbreak{}Marvel}. Irenaeus: "what great thing or what sign should have been in this, that a young woman conceiving by a man should bring forth — a thing which happens to all women that produce offspring?" Justin asks why God would give "a sign which is not common to all the first-born sons" (Dial. 84). \texttt{consensus}, because the rival grants it: its point is that the sign need not be a marvel, not that an ordinary conception is one (Rhodea 64).

{\small\begin{verbatim}

            Reference.work Bib.rhodeaDidMatthewConceive (Bib.Locus.page 64)],
        tradition := Tradition.christianTypological,
        confidence := Confidence.consensus } }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{isaianic\allowbreak{}Signs\allowbreak{}Are\allowbreak{}Ordinary\allowbreak{}Events\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{isaianic\allowbreak{}Signs\allowbreak{}Are\allowbreak{}Ordinary\allowbreak{}Events}. What the text says: Rhodea lists non-supernatural signs "including elsewhere in Isaiah" (64), and Wegner a sign as "a common, everyday occurrence that has significance because of what it means, foretells, or predicts" (470). Rydelnik, as Postell reports him (474), grants the first of them: Isaiah's children are the "signs and wonders" of 8:18.

{\small\begin{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{sign\allowbreak{}Dated\allowbreak{}By\allowbreak{}AChilds\allowbreak{}Infancy\allowbreak{}Cited}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\texttt{sign\allowbreak{}Dated\allowbreak{}By\allowbreak{}AChilds\allowbreak{}Infancy}. What 7:16 says, and common ground about which child is left open: Wegner reads it of the child of 7:14 (477), Rydelnik of Shear-jashub (Postell 474), and Compton of a child whose infancy only measures the time (14).

                { book := Book.isaiah, chapter := 7, verse := 16 }],
