$P_{21}$ & Good works are the fruit and evidence of saving faith, not its ground & theological & Reformed Protestant, well supported & \cite[XI.2]{westminster-confession-1647}; \cite[III.xvi.1]{calvin-institutes-1960} \\
$P_{22}$ & James 2:24 is compatible with Paul, using ‘justify’ in a different sense & interpretive & Reformed Protestant, disputed & Jas 2:24; \cite[ad loc. Jas 2:24]{moo-james-2000}; \cite[III.xvii.11]{calvin-institutes-1960} \\
$P_{23}$ & Scripture does not contradict itself & theological & Reformed Protestant, well supported & \cite[I.vii]{calvin-institutes-1960}; 2 Pet 3:15-16; \cite[ad loc. 2 Pet 3:16]{schreiner-peter-jude-2003} \\
$P_{24}$ & Justification is by faith alone & theological & Reformed Protestant, well supported & \cite[III.xi.1]{calvin-institutes-1960}; \cite{schreiner-faith-alone-2015}; \cite{mannermaa-christ-present-faith-2005} \\
$P_{25}$ & Salvation is by grace, a gift and not wages owed & theological & Reformed Protestant, well supported & \cite[III.xi–xviii]{calvin-institutes-1960}; 1 Pet 1:18-19; \cite[ad loc. 1 Pet 1:18]{jobes-1-peter-2005} \\
$P_{26}$ & Salvation is not by works: no work is its ground & theological & Reformed Protestant, well supported & \cite[III.xi–xviii]{calvin-institutes-1960}; \cite{schreiner-faith-alone-2015} \\
$P_{27}$ & Salvation is received through faith & theological & Reformed Protestant, well supported & \cite[III.xi–xviii]{calvin-institutes-1960}; 1 Pet 1:3-5; \cite[ad loc. 1 Pet 1:5]{jobes-1-peter-2005} \\
$P_{28}$ & Justification is forensic only: pardon and imputation, not infusion & theological & Reformed Protestant, well supported & \cite[XI.1]{westminster-confession-1647}; \cite[III.xi.2]{calvin-institutes-1960} \\
$P_{29}$ & Second Temple Judaism: in by grace, staying in by works & historical & critical scholarship, disputed & \cite{sanders-paul-palestinian-judaism-1977} \\
$P_{30}$ & Works performed in grace merit an increase of justification & theological & Roman Catholic, well supported & \cite[Trent, Session VI (1547), Decree on Justification, ch. 16]{tanner-decrees-1990} \\
$P_{31}$ & Justification is not remission of sins only, but renewal of the inward man & theological & Roman Catholic, disputed & \cite[Trent, Session VI (1547), Decree on Justification, ch. 7]{tanner-decrees-1990}; \cite[Trent, Session VI (1547), canon 11]{tanner-decrees-1990} \\
$P_{32}$ & Justification and sanctification are inseparable but distinct & theological & Reformed Protestant, disputed & \cite[XIII.1]{westminster-confession-1647}; \cite[XI.1]{westminster-confession-1647}; \cite[III.xi.6]{calvin-institutes-1960} \\
$P_{33}$ & The inward renewal of the justified grows as they do good works in grace & theological & Roman Catholic, well supported & \cite[Trent, Session VI (1547), Decree on Justification, ch. 10]{tanner-decrees-1990}; \cite[XIII.1]{westminster-confession-1647} \\
$P_{34}$ & John 6:28–29 — the work God requires is that you believe in him whom he sent & textual & Christian, hist.-gramm., consensus & John 6:28-29; \cite[ad loc. John 6:29]{calvin-commentary-john-1847}; \cite[cap. 6, lect. 3, n. 901]{aquinas-commentary-john-2012} \\
$P_{35}$ & John 3:16–18, 3:36, 5:24, 20:31 — eternal life through believing in the Son\textsuperscript{\dag} & textual & Christian, hist.-gramm., consensus & John 3:16-18; John 3:36; John 5:24; John 20:31 \\
$P_{36}$ & The believing of John 6:29 is trust, which brings nothing and receives Christ & interpretive & Reformed Protestant, disputed & \cite[ad loc. John 6:29]{calvin-commentary-john-1847}; John 6:28-29 \\
$P_{37}$ & The believing of John 6:29 is faith living through charity, the source of works & interpretive & Roman Catholic, disputed & \cite[cap. 6, lect. 3, n. 901]{aquinas-commentary-john-2012}; \cite[Trent, Session VI (1547), Decree on Justification, ch. 7]{tanner-decrees-1990} \\
$P_{38}$ & Galatians 3:11–12 — none is justified by the law; the law is not of faith & textual & Christian, hist.-gramm., consensus & Gal 3:11-12; \cite[ad loc. Gal 3:11]{luther-commentary-galatians-1998} \\
$P_{39}$ & Love of God and neighbour is what the law commands & textual & Christian, hist.-gramm., consensus & Deut 6:5; Matt 22:37-40; \cite[ad loc. Gal 3:12]{luther-commentary-galatians-1998} \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\noindent\footnotesize \textsuperscript{\dag} grounded in scripture alone: the reading is assumed, not argued for.
\normalsize

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{trent\allowbreak{}Against\allowbreak{}Faith\allowbreak{}Alone}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{Every Reformed strand's step is contested, by Trent.} Trent grants what each strand reads — Romans 3:28 and Galatians 2:16, that Jesus' words save, that salvation is through grace — and anathematises the conclusion each draws: justification by faith alone (Session VI, canon 9).

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def trentAgainstFaithAlone : Source :=
  {
    primary :=
      Reference.work Bib.tannerDecrees
        (Bib.Locus.sectionRef "Trent, Session VI (1547), canon 9"),
    tradition := Tradition.romanCatholic, confidence := Confidence.disputed }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{trent\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Merit}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Trent's own step, from merit to the denial of "not by works". Rated \texttt{consensus}: the step is conceded on both sides — it is \emph{why} the Reformed deny merit — and the dispute is over its premise, not its inference.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def trentOnMerit : Source :=
  {
    primary :=
      Reference.work Bib.tannerDecrees
        (Bib.Locus.sectionRef
          "Trent, Session VI (1547), Decree on Justification, ch. 16"),
    tradition := Tradition.romanCatholic, confidence := Confidence.consensus }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{melanchthon\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Luke7}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Where Melanchthon reads Luke 7:47 by 7:50, in the \emph{Apology of the Augsburg Confession} (1531): the part of Article IV on love and the fulfilling of the law. The \emph{Concordia Triglotta} numbers it Article III, 31–34; the reference is given by article and topic because paragraph numbers differ between editions.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def melanchthonOnLuke7 : Bib.Locus :=
  Bib.Locus.sectionRef
    "Apology IV, on love and the fulfilling of the law: Luke 7:47–50"
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{campbell\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Deliverance}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The apocalyptic reading's step, from Christ's faithfulness and God's deliverance to the denial that faith is the condition. Rated \texttt{plausible}: no source cited here grants both grounds and affirms faith as the condition of justification.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def campbellOnDeliverance : Source :=
  { primary := Reference.work Bib.campbellDeliveranceOfGod,
    supporting := [Reference.work Bib.martynGalatians],
    tradition := Tradition.criticalScholarship,
    confidence := Confidence.plausible }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{reformed\allowbreak{}Works\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}Law\allowbreak{}Source}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The Reformed reading of ἔργα νόμου, cited to the tradition that holds it and to its modern defenders against the New Perspective. Disputed precisely because the New Perspective denies it; see \texttt{new\allowbreak{}Perspective\allowbreak{}Cite}.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def reformedWorksOfLawSource : Source
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{base\allowbreak{}Cite}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Citations shared by every package: the prooftexts and the hermeneutical premises, which none of the three positions disputes the wording of.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def baseCite : Claim → AtomMeta
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{reformed\allowbreak{}Cite}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The Reformed reading of the disputed premise.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def reformedCite : Claim → AtomMeta :=
  fun x =>
    match x with
    | Claim.worksOfLawMeansWorksGenerally =>
      {
        label :=
          "Paul's ἔργα νόμου denotes human works in general, not boundary markers",
        kind := PremiseKind.linguistic,
        source := reformedWorksOfLawSource }
    | c => baseCite c
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{new\allowbreak{}Perspective\allowbreak{}Cite}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The New Perspective's reading of the disputed premise: ἔργα νόμου denotes the covenant boundary markers, so Paul is not addressing human effort in general.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def newPerspectiveCite : Claim → AtomMeta
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{subjective\allowbreak{}Genitive\allowbreak{}Cite}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Hays's reading of the genitive: πίστις Χριστοῦ is Christ's own faithfulness. Everything else as \texttt{base\allowbreak{}Cite}.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def subjectiveGenitiveCite : Claim → AtomMeta
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{law\allowbreak{}Observant\allowbreak{}Luke\allowbreak{}Cite}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Jervell's reading of Luke-Acts: what Acts 15 withholds from gentiles is Israel's law as a mark of belonging, and the law is not refused as a means of salvation. Everything else as \texttt{base\allowbreak{}Cite}.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def lawObservantLukeCite : Claim → AtomMeta
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{critical\allowbreak{}Authorship\allowbreak{}Cite}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The critical view of authorship: Ephesians and Titus are not by Paul, and 1 and 2 Peter not by Peter. Each atom is cited to a scholar who holds that view of that letter, with Ehrman for the case across all four. Marshall's mediating view of the Pastorals — written by Paul's circle after his death, without intent to deceive — is cited with the critical side, because it too denies that Paul wrote Titus.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def criticalAuthorshipCite : Claim → AtomMeta
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{critics\allowbreak{}Cite}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The New Perspective's critics: Second Temple Judaism was not uniformly covenantal nomism, because final vindication according to works was widely held. Gathercole primary, the \emph{Variegated Nomism} volume in support.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def criticsCite : Claim → AtomMeta :=
  fun x =>
    match x with
    | Claim.secondTempleCovenantalNomism =>
      {
        label :=
          "Second Temple Judaism also held final vindication according to works",
        kind := PremiseKind.historical,
        source :=
          { primary := Reference.work Bib.gathercoleWhereIsBoasting,
            supporting := [Reference.work Bib.carsonVariegatedNomism1],
            tradition := Tradition.reformedProtestant,
            confidence := Confidence.disputed } }
    | c => reformedCite c
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{tridentine\allowbreak{}Cite}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The Tridentine reading of what justification is: "not remission of sins merely, but also the sanctification and renewal of the inward man". Everything else as \texttt{base\allowbreak{}Cite}.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def tridentineCite : Claim → AtomMeta :=
  fun x =>
    match x with
    | Claim.justificationIsForensicOnly =>
      { label := "Justification is not forensic only: it renews the inward man",
        kind := PremiseKind.theological,
        source :=
          {
            primary :=
              Reference.work Bib.tannerDecrees
                (Bib.Locus.sectionRef
                  "Trent, Session VI (1547), Decree on Justification, ch. 7"),
            tradition := Tradition.romanCatholic,
            confidence := Confidence.wellSupported } }
    | c => baseCite c
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{finnish\allowbreak{}Cite}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The Finnish reading of Luther: justification is not forensic only, because Christ himself is present in faith and is the believer's righteousness. Everything else as \texttt{reformed\allowbreak{}Cite}.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def finnishCite : Claim → AtomMeta :=
  fun x =>
    match x with
    | Claim.justificationIsForensicOnly =>
      {
        label :=
          "Justification is not forensic only: Christ is present in faith",
        kind := PremiseKind.theological,
        source :=
          { primary := Reference.work Bib.mannermaaChristPresentInFaith,
            tradition := Tradition.criticalScholarship,
            confidence := Confidence.disputed } }
    | c => reformedCite c
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{apocalyptic\allowbreak{}Cite}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The apocalyptic reading: the genitive is subjective, and it is Campbell's reason for reading it so that the citation records.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def apocalypticCite : Claim → AtomMeta
\end{verbatim}}
\subsection*{Arguments.SolaFide.Lines — the strands, and what they share}
Three strands deliver justification by faith alone, by different words from different speakers: Paul, Jesus, and Peter at Jerusalem. A fourth line answers James, and a fifth — the apocalyptic reading — denies what the strands deliver. What every package holds in common is collected here too, so that a variant package is a named difference rather than a retyped list.

\paragraph*{Lines of reason}
\leavevmode\par
Three strands deliver the conclusion, and a fourth line answers James. The James harmonisation is deliberately \emph{not} a strand: it does not reach \texttt{justification\allowbreak{}By\allowbreak{}Faith\allowbreak{}Alone} by an independent route, it supplies a premise the closing step needs. So it contributes its grounds and its step to the case without being one of the lines \texttt{case\allowbreak{}Of} treats as converging.

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{pauline\allowbreak{}To\allowbreak{}Faith\allowbreak{}Alone}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The Pauline strand.} From Romans and Galatians to justification by faith alone, read from Galatians 2:16 as the centre of the letter's polemic.

Each premise does its own work. Galatians 2:16 excludes works of the law; read as an objective genitive, it also names faith in Christ as the means of justification. The polemic says what is being refused: circumcision \emph{added to} faith as a requirement. That is where \emph{alone} comes from. The Galatians already believed, and adding a work to their faith is what Paul says severs them from Christ (5:4). Only the ἔργα νόμου premise lets circumcision stand for works in general rather than for one ethnic marker.

\[(P_{2} \land P_{3} \land P_{8} \land P_{7} \land P_{6}) \rightarrow P_{24}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{dominical\allowbreak{}To\allowbreak{}Faith\allowbreak{}Alone}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The dominical strand.} From Jesus' own words at Luke 7:50 to justification by faith alone, by way of the lexical premise about σῴζω and the reading of 7:47 that answers "she loved much": her love shows her forgiveness and does not earn it, so what Jesus names as saving her is her faith. Independent of Paul, and of the ἔργα νόμου dispute.

\[(P_{17} \land P_{18} \land P_{19}) \rightarrow P_{24}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{apostolic\allowbreak{}To\allowbreak{}Faith\allowbreak{}Alone}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The apostolic strand.} From Peter's speech at the Jerusalem council to justification by faith alone, by way of the disputed premise about the yoke.

The council answers the same demand Galatians answers: circumcision and the law of Moses as a condition of salvation (Acts 15:1, 15:5). Peter's reply names faith as what cleansed the gentiles' hearts and grace as how Jew and gentile alike are saved, and refuses the yoke. If the yoke is the whole law as a condition of salvation, what is left is faith, and grace.

\[(P_{9} \land P_{10}) \rightarrow P_{24}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{james\allowbreak{}Harmonisation}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The harmonisation of James: because James's target is barren faith, and works are the fruit of saving faith rather than its ground, James 2:24 does not contradict Paul. Derived rather than assumed.

\[(P_{20} \land P_{21}) \rightarrow P_{22}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{sola\allowbreak{}Fide}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The conclusion}, in its three parts: salvation is by grace, not by works, and through faith. Stated as three atoms rather than one so that a position can be shown to hold some parts and deny another — which the apocalyptic reading and the Tridentine position each do, over different parts.

\[P_{25} \land P_{26} \land P_{27}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{grace\allowbreak{}Not\allowbreak{}Works}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The first two parts: by grace, and not by works. What the apocalyptic reading keeps.

\[P_{25} \land P_{26}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{to\allowbreak{}Grace}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\emph{By grace}, from the texts that say so: a gift through faith (Ephesians 2:8), a gift and not wages (Romans 4:4–5), mercy (Titus 3:5). Neither a strand nor the answer to James is needed for this part.

\[(P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5}) \rightarrow P_{25}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{to\allowbreak{}Not\allowbreak{}By\allowbreak{}Works}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\emph{Not by works}, from the same texts, which say "not of works" in terms — provided James 2:24 is answered, and scripture does not contradict itself. No strand is needed for this part either; what it needs, and grace does not, is an answer to James.

\[(P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5} \land P_{22} \land P_{23}) \rightarrow P_{26}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{to\allowbreak{}Through\allowbreak{}Faith}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\emph{Through faith}, from justification by faith alone and Ephesians 2:8. This is the only part of the conclusion the three strands are needed for.

\[(P_{24} \land P_{1}) \rightarrow P_{27}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{pauline\allowbreak{}Line}} --- Pauline strand (ἔργα νόμου, πίστις Χριστοῦ)\par\nobreak\noindent
The Pauline line. Its grounds are the reading of Galatians as a polemic and the two disputed lexical premises, about ἔργα νόμου and about πίστις Χριστοῦ. The prooftexts its step reads are shared with every other package, which is exactly why denying a lexical premise does not cost the argument its prooftexts.

The genitive dispute is a premise \emph{inside} this strand, not a third strand. Every πίστις Χριστοῦ text sets it against νόμος, so a route through the genitive still passes through what ἔργα νόμου denotes. A line on the genitive whose grounds omitted the ἔργα νόμου premise would misdescribe Paul.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{8} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{6} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{7} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & (P_{2} \land P_{3} \land P_{8} \land P_{7} \land P_{6}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{24}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{dominical\allowbreak{}Line}} --- Dominical strand (σῴζω at Luke 7:50)\par\nobreak\noindent
The dominical line, resting on Jesus' words rather than Paul's. Its distinctive grounds are the other lexical premise, and the reading of 7:47 that keeps the woman's love from being the ground of her forgiveness.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{18} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{19} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & (P_{17} \land P_{18} \land P_{19}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{24}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{apostolic\allowbreak{}Line}} --- Apostolic strand (Acts 15:7–11)\par\nobreak\noindent
The apostolic line, resting on Peter as Luke reports him rather than on Paul or on Jesus. Its distinctive ground is the premise about the yoke.

It is logically independent of the Pauline strand: no premise is shared. It is not dialectically independent. A reader who takes ἔργα νόμου as Israel's boundary markers will read Peter's yoke the same way, as Jervell does, so the two premises tend to be won or lost together. What the encoding shows is that they are two premises about two texts, and an opponent has to answer both.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{10} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & (P_{9} \land P_{10}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{24}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{james\allowbreak{}Line}} --- Harmonisation of James 2:24\par\nobreak\noindent
The James line: not a route to the conclusion but the answer to the one text that stands against it.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{20} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{21} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & (P_{20} \land P_{21}) \rightarrow P_{22} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{22}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{deliverance\allowbreak{}Not\allowbreak{}Faith\allowbreak{}Alone}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The apocalyptic reading} (Martyn, Campbell). If πίστις Χριστοῦ is Christ's own faithfulness, and δικαιοσύνη θεοῦ is God's act of deliverance, then faith is not the condition on which justification is granted. So justification by faith alone, in the sense the Reformed and New Perspective packages share, is denied. Grace and "not by works" are not denied: the reading rejects the condition, not the gift.

\[(\lnot P_{7} \land P_{16}) \rightarrow \lnot P_{24}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{deliverance\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Grace}}\par\nobreak\noindent
What the apocalyptic reading keeps. If δικαιοσύνη θεοῦ is God's act of deliverance, it is sheer gift, conditioned on nothing a person does: by grace, and not by works. Campbell's inference, and his reason for calling the reading more gracious than the one it replaces.

\[P_{16} \rightarrow P_{25} \land P_{26}\]
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{apocalyptic\allowbreak{}Line}} --- Apocalyptic reading (δικαιοσύνη θεοῦ as deliverance)\par\nobreak\noindent
The apocalyptic line: a rival route from Paul, delivering the denial of what the two Reformed strands deliver.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & \lnot P_{7} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{16} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & (\lnot P_{7} \land P_{16}) \rightarrow \lnot P_{24} \\
  \vdash \quad & \lnot P_{24}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{sanders\allowbreak{}Line}} --- Covenantal nomism (Sanders, Dunn)\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The New Perspective's reason} for its reading of ἔργα νόμου. If Second Temple Judaism was covenantal nomism — in by grace, staying in by works — then Paul's opponents were not seeking to earn salvation, and what he refuses is not works as such but the marks that kept gentiles out: the boundary markers. The ground is Sanders'; the inference is Dunn's, and is cited as his.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{29} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{29} \rightarrow \lnot P_{6} \\
  \vdash \quad & \lnot P_{6}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{critics\allowbreak{}Line}} --- Variegated nomism (Gathercole, Carson et al.)\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The critics' reply.} If Second Temple Judaism also held final vindication according to works, then the demand Galatians refuses — circumcision added to faith as a requirement — is a demand for obedience, and what Paul refuses in refusing it is works as such. So the Reformed reading of ἔργα νόμου is not assumed but derived: from the reading of Galatians as a polemic, and from a historical claim about Judaism that is argued on the evidence.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & \lnot P_{29} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{8} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & (\lnot P_{29} \land P_{8}) \rightarrow P_{6} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{6}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{jervell\allowbreak{}Line}} --- Law-observant Luke (Jervell)\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{Jervell's reading of the yoke.} If Luke presents the Jewish believers as keeping the law (Acts 21:20–24), and the decree as laying part of it on gentiles (Acts 15:20–21), then what Peter refuses at 15:10 is not the law as a condition of salvation but the whole of Israel's law laid on gentiles. The step is contested: Bruce grants both texts and reads the yoke as the law taken as a condition of salvation.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{11} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{11} \rightarrow \lnot P_{10} \\
  \vdash \quad & \lnot P_{10}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{trent\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Increase}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Trent's step from its definition to merit: if justification \emph{is} the renewal of the inward man, and that renewal grows through good works, then good works increase justification (Session VI, ch. 10 and canon 24). Rated \texttt{consensus}, as \texttt{trent\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Merit} is: the Reformed deny the definition, not the inference, and canon 24 condemns the Reformed alternative by name — that works are "merely the fruits and signs of Justification obtained".

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def trentOnIncrease : Source :=
  {
    primary :=
      Reference.work Bib.tannerDecrees
        (Bib.Locus.sectionRef "Trent, Session VI (1547), canon 24"),
    supporting :=
      [Reference.work Bib.tannerDecrees
          (Bib.Locus.sectionRef
            "Trent, Session VI (1547), Decree on Justification, ch. 10")],
    tradition := Tradition.romanCatholic, confidence := Confidence.consensus }
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{tridentine\allowbreak{}Line}} --- Trent (justification as renewal)\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{Trent's line.} From its definition of justification — the renewal of the inward man, not remission of sins only — and the growth of that renewal through good works, to merit: good works increase justification (Session VI, ch. 10, canon 24). The Reformed grant the growth and deny the definition, so the step carries the objection only as far as the definition does.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{31} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{33} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & (P_{31} \land P_{33}) \rightarrow P_{30} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{30}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{trent\allowbreak{}Definition\allowbreak{}Steps}}\par\nobreak\noindent
What Trent's definition rules out: justification as forensic only (canon 11), and justification as distinct from the sanctification that follows it.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{31} \rightarrow \lnot P_{28} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{31} \rightarrow \lnot P_{32} \\
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{prooftexts}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The prooftexts the strands read. Shared by every Reformed package, and by the New Perspective.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{1} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{2} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{3} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{4} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & P_{5} \\
  \text{(6)} \quad & P_{17} \\
  \text{(7)} \quad & P_{9} \\
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{shared\allowbreak{}Grounds}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Everything the strands share: the prooftexts, the grounds of the James harmonisation, and scripture's self-consistency.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{1} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{2} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{3} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{4} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & P_{5} \\
  \text{(6)} \quad & P_{17} \\
  \text{(7)} \quad & P_{9} \\
  \text{(8)} \quad & P_{20} \\
  \text{(9)} \quad & P_{21} \\
  \text{(10)} \quad & P_{23} \\
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{shared\allowbreak{}Grounds\allowbreak{}Without\allowbreak{}James}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The same, with James unanswered.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{1} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{2} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{3} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{4} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & P_{5} \\
  \text{(6)} \quad & P_{17} \\
  \text{(7)} \quad & P_{9} \\
  \text{(8)} \quad & P_{23} \\
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{conclusion\allowbreak{}Steps}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The three steps to the three parts of the conclusion, one each.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5}) \rightarrow P_{25} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5} \land P_{22} \land P_{23}) \rightarrow P_{26} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & (P_{24} \land P_{1}) \rightarrow P_{27} \\
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{closing\allowbreak{}Steps}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The steps that close the argument: the James harmonisation, then the steps to the parts of the conclusion.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & (P_{20} \land P_{21}) \rightarrow P_{22} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5}) \rightarrow P_{25} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5} \land P_{22} \land P_{23}) \rightarrow P_{26} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & (P_{24} \land P_{1}) \rightarrow P_{27} \\
\end{align*}
\subsection*{Arguments.SolaFide.Packages — the positions, and the variants}
The positions — Reformed, New Perspective, subjective genitive, law-observant Luke, Finnish, critical authorship, apocalyptic, Tridentine — and reduced Reformed packages, each missing a named premise. Whether a premise is load-bearing is shown by removing it and then either establishing the conclusion anyway or naming a countermodel.

\paragraph*{Packages}
\leavevmode\par
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{reformed}} --- Reformed (sola fide)\par\nobreak\noindent
The classical Protestant position: all three strands, and every disputed premise they rest on — ἔργα νόμου and πίστις Χριστοῦ in Paul, σῴζω in Luke, the yoke in Acts.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{8} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{6} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{7} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{18} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & P_{19} \\
  \text{(6)} \quad & P_{10} \\
  \text{(7)} \quad & P_{1} \\
  \text{(8)} \quad & P_{2} \\
  \text{(9)} \quad & P_{3} \\
  \text{(10)} \quad & P_{4} \\
  \text{(11)} \quad & P_{5} \\
  \text{(12)} \quad & P_{17} \\
  \text{(13)} \quad & P_{9} \\
  \text{(14)} \quad & P_{20} \\
  \text{(15)} \quad & P_{21} \\
  \text{(16)} \quad & P_{23} \\
  \text{(17)} \quad & (P_{2} \land P_{3} \land P_{8} \land P_{7} \land P_{6}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(18)} \quad & (P_{17} \land P_{18} \land P_{19}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(19)} \quad & (P_{9} \land P_{10}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(20)} \quad & (P_{20} \land P_{21}) \rightarrow P_{22} \\
  \text{(21)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5}) \rightarrow P_{25} \\
  \text{(22)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5} \land P_{22} \land P_{23}) \rightarrow P_{26} \\
  \text{(23)} \quad & (P_{24} \land P_{1}) \rightarrow P_{27} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{25} \land P_{26} \land P_{27}
\end{align*}
\emph{No premise here rests on scripture alone.}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{dominical\allowbreak{}Without\allowbreak{}Sozo}} --- Dominical strand (σῴζω at Luke 7:50)\par\nobreak\noindent
The dominical line without the σῴζω premise. The reading of 7:47 stays: it is not the premise being tested.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{19} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & (P_{17} \land P_{18} \land P_{19}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{24}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{pauline\allowbreak{}Without\allowbreak{}Works\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}Law}} --- Pauline strand (ἔργα νόμου, πίστις Χριστοῦ)\par\nobreak\noindent
The Pauline line without the ἔργα νόμου premise.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{8} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{7} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & (P_{2} \land P_{3} \land P_{8} \land P_{7} \land P_{6}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{24}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{pauline\allowbreak{}Without\allowbreak{}Pistis\allowbreak{}Christou}} --- Pauline strand (ἔργα νόμου, πίστις Χριστοῦ)\par\nobreak\noindent
The Pauline line without the objective genitive.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{8} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{6} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & (P_{2} \land P_{3} \land P_{8} \land P_{7} \land P_{6}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{24}
\end{align*}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{new\allowbreak{}Perspective}} --- New Perspective on Paul\par\nobreak\noindent
The New Perspective on Paul: it denies the traditional reading of ἔργα νόμου while accepting justification by faith, which is what Dunn and Wright actually hold. It keeps the objective genitive, as Dunn does, so the one Pauline ground it denies is the ἔργα νόμου premise.

The denial is not assumed: \texttt{sanders\allowbreak{}Line} derives it from covenantal nomism, so the package rests on Sanders' account of Judaism and Dunn's inference from it, which is where its critics engage it.

It takes no position on the yoke of Acts 15:10: the apostolic line keeps its step and loses its ground, so the package neither grants nor denies the premise on the New Perspective's behalf.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{8} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{7} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{29} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{18} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & P_{19} \\
  \text{(6)} \quad & P_{1} \\
  \text{(7)} \quad & P_{2} \\
  \text{(8)} \quad & P_{3} \\
  \text{(9)} \quad & P_{4} \\
  \text{(10)} \quad & P_{5} \\
  \text{(11)} \quad & P_{17} \\
  \text{(12)} \quad & P_{9} \\
  \text{(13)} \quad & P_{20} \\
  \text{(14)} \quad & P_{21} \\
  \text{(15)} \quad & P_{23} \\
  \text{(16)} \quad & (P_{2} \land P_{3} \land P_{8} \land P_{7} \land P_{6}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(17)} \quad & P_{29} \rightarrow \lnot P_{6} \\
  \text{(18)} \quad & (P_{17} \land P_{18} \land P_{19}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(19)} \quad & (P_{9} \land P_{10}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(20)} \quad & (P_{20} \land P_{21}) \rightarrow P_{22} \\
  \text{(21)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5}) \rightarrow P_{25} \\
  \text{(22)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5} \land P_{22} \land P_{23}) \rightarrow P_{26} \\
  \text{(23)} \quad & (P_{24} \land P_{1}) \rightarrow P_{27} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{25} \land P_{26} \land P_{27}
\end{align*}
\emph{No premise here rests on scripture alone.}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{finnish}} --- Finnish reading of Luther (Mannermaa)\par\nobreak\noindent
The Finnish reading of Luther (Mannermaa): the Reformed case, with the forensic account of justification denied. Christ himself is present in faith and is the believer's righteousness, so justification is not a declaration only.

On what justification \emph{is}, this sides with Trent against the Reformed account; on sola fide it sides with the Reformed. The encoding shows both, and shows that the two questions are independent.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{8} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{6} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{7} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{18} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & P_{19} \\
  \text{(6)} \quad & P_{10} \\
  \text{(7)} \quad & P_{1} \\
  \text{(8)} \quad & P_{2} \\
  \text{(9)} \quad & P_{3} \\
  \text{(10)} \quad & P_{4} \\
  \text{(11)} \quad & P_{5} \\
  \text{(12)} \quad & P_{17} \\
  \text{(13)} \quad & P_{9} \\
  \text{(14)} \quad & P_{20} \\
  \text{(15)} \quad & P_{21} \\
  \text{(16)} \quad & P_{23} \\
  \text{(17)} \quad & (P_{2} \land P_{3} \land P_{8} \land P_{7} \land P_{6}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(18)} \quad & (P_{17} \land P_{18} \land P_{19}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(19)} \quad & (P_{9} \land P_{10}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(20)} \quad & (P_{20} \land P_{21}) \rightarrow P_{22} \\
  \text{(21)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5}) \rightarrow P_{25} \\
  \text{(22)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5} \land P_{22} \land P_{23}) \rightarrow P_{26} \\
  \text{(23)} \quad & (P_{24} \land P_{1}) \rightarrow P_{27} \\
  \text{(24)} \quad & \lnot P_{28} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{25} \land P_{26} \land P_{27}
\end{align*}
\emph{No premise here rests on scripture alone.}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{subjective\allowbreak{}Genitive}} --- Subjective genitive (πίστις Χριστοῦ as Christ's faithfulness)\par\nobreak\noindent
The subjective genitive (Hays): πίστις Χριστοῦ in Galatians 2:16 is Christ's own faithfulness, so the verse does not name the believer's faith as the means of justification. Everything else in the Reformed case is granted.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{8} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{6} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & \lnot P_{7} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{18} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & P_{19} \\
  \text{(6)} \quad & P_{10} \\
  \text{(7)} \quad & P_{1} \\

  \text{(18)} \quad & (P_{17} \land P_{18} \land P_{19}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(19)} \quad & (P_{9} \land P_{10}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(20)} \quad & (P_{20} \land P_{21}) \rightarrow P_{22} \\
  \text{(21)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5}) \rightarrow P_{25} \\
  \text{(22)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5} \land P_{22} \land P_{23}) \rightarrow P_{26} \\
  \text{(23)} \quad & (P_{24} \land P_{1}) \rightarrow P_{27} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{25} \land P_{26} \land P_{27}
\end{align*}
\emph{No premise here rests on scripture alone.}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{law\allowbreak{}Observant\allowbreak{}Luke}} --- Law-observant Luke (the yoke as Israel's law for gentiles)\par\nobreak\noindent
Luke as law-observant (Jervell): the yoke Acts 15:10 refuses is Israel's law laid on gentiles as a mark of belonging, not the law as a condition of salvation. Everything else in the Reformed case is granted. The rival the apostolic strand was written against.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{8} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{6} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{7} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{18} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & P_{19} \\
  \text{(6)} \quad & \lnot P_{10} \\

  \text{(9)} \quad & P_{3} \\
  \text{(10)} \quad & P_{4} \\
  \text{(11)} \quad & P_{5} \\
  \text{(12)} \quad & P_{17} \\
  \text{(13)} \quad & P_{9} \\
  \text{(14)} \quad & P_{20} \\
  \text{(15)} \quad & P_{21} \\
  \text{(16)} \quad & P_{23} \\
  \text{(17)} \quad & (P_{2} \land P_{3} \land P_{8} \land P_{7} \land P_{6}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(18)} \quad & (P_{17} \land P_{18} \land P_{19}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(19)} \quad & (P_{9} \land P_{10}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(20)} \quad & (P_{20} \land P_{21}) \rightarrow P_{22} \\
  \text{(21)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5}) \rightarrow P_{25} \\
  \text{(22)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5} \land P_{22} \land P_{23}) \rightarrow P_{26} \\
  \text{(23)} \quad & (P_{24} \land P_{1}) \rightarrow P_{27} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{25} \land P_{26} \land P_{27}
\end{align*}
\emph{No premise here rests on scripture alone.}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{critical\allowbreak{}Authorship}} --- Reformed, with the critical view of authorship\par\nobreak\noindent
The Reformed case granted every critical conclusion about authorship: Ephesians and Titus not by Paul, 1 and 2 Peter not by Peter.

Nothing else changes, and that is the point. The case reads these letters as canonical scripture, bound together by \texttt{scripture\allowbreak{}Self\allowbreak{}Consistent}, not as the testimony of a named apostle. So conceding their authorship costs the argument nothing — while it would cost an argument that cited Ephesians 2:9 as evidence of what \emph{Paul} meant by "works", which is why \texttt{works\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}Law\allowbreak{}Means\allowbreak{}Works\allowbreak{}Generally} leans on Romans instead.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{8} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{6} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{7} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{18} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & P_{19} \\

  \text{(14)} \quad & P_{20} \\
  \text{(15)} \quad & P_{21} \\
  \text{(16)} \quad & P_{23} \\
  \text{(17)} \quad & (P_{2} \land P_{3} \land P_{8} \land P_{7} \land P_{6}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(18)} \quad & (P_{17} \land P_{18} \land P_{19}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\
  \text{(19)} \quad & (P_{9} \land P_{10}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\

  \text{(22)} \quad & (P_{1} \land P_{4} \land P_{5} \land P_{22} \land P_{23}) \rightarrow P_{26} \\
  \text{(23)} \quad & (P_{24} \land P_{1}) \rightarrow P_{27} \\
  \text{(24)} \quad & \lnot P_{12} \\
  \text{(25)} \quad & \lnot P_{13} \\
  \text{(26)} \quad & \lnot P_{14} \\
  \text{(27)} \quad & \lnot P_{15} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{25} \land P_{26} \land P_{27}
\end{align*}
\emph{No premise here rests on scripture alone.}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{apocalyptic}} --- Apocalyptic reading of Paul (Martyn, Campbell)\par\nobreak\noindent
The apocalyptic reading of Paul (Martyn, Campbell). It rejects the traditional reading's framing of justification and also the New Perspective's. The question is neither how an individual is accepted nor how Jew and gentile are related, but God's deliverance of the world in Christ.

It shares Galatians with the Reformed reading — the text of 2:16 and the circumcision polemic — and closes with the same step to salvation. It does not share the dominical or apostolic strands, which neither Martyn nor Campbell argues from; to grant it Luke 7:50 or Acts 15 would be to encode a position nobody holds.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & \lnot P_{7} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{16} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{3} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{8} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & (\lnot P_{7} \land P_{16}) \rightarrow \lnot P_{24} \\
  \text{(6)} \quad & P_{16} \rightarrow P_{25} \land P_{26} \\
  \text{(7)} \quad & (P_{24} \land P_{1}) \rightarrow P_{27} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{25} \land P_{26} \land P_{27}
\end{align*}
\emph{No premise here rests on scripture alone.}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{apocalyptic\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Grace\allowbreak{}And\allowbreak{}Works}} --- Apocalyptic reading, on grace and works only\par\nobreak\noindent
The apocalyptic reading, asked only about the first two parts of the conclusion: by grace, and not by works.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & \lnot P_{7} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{16} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{3} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{8} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & (\lnot P_{7} \land P_{16}) \rightarrow \lnot P_{24} \\
  \text{(6)} \quad & P_{16} \rightarrow P_{25} \land P_{26} \\
  \text{(7)} \quad & (P_{24} \land P_{1}) \rightarrow P_{27} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{25} \land P_{26}
\end{align*}
\emph{No premise here rests on scripture alone.}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{tridentine\allowbreak{}Premises}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Trent's premises, given the line that carries its definition to merit: four prooftexts it shares with the Reformed, scripture's self-consistency, what its definition rules out, and the step from merit to the denial of "not by works" (ch. 16).

Not built from the Reformed lines, and that is the point: it shares four prooftexts with them and nothing else, so \texttt{case\allowbreak{}Of} would misdescribe it.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def tridentinePremises : Line Claim → List (Formula Claim) :=
  fun line =>
    [p Claim.ephesians2_8_9,
            p Claim.romans3_28,
            p Claim.galatians2_16,
            p Claim.titus3_5,
            p Claim.scriptureSelfConsistent] ++
          line.premises ++
        trentDefinitionSteps ++
      [p Claim.worksMeritIncreaseOfJustification 🡒
          notP Claim.salvationNotByWorks]
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{tridentine}} --- Tridentine (Council of Trent, Session VI)\par\nobreak\noindent
The Tridentine position: justification is the renewal of the inward man, that renewal grows through good works, so works performed in grace merit an increase of justification — which denies the second part of the conclusion, salvation not by works. The same definition denies that justification is forensic only.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{1} \\
  \text{(2)} \quad & P_{2} \\
  \text{(3)} \quad & P_{3} \\
  \text{(4)} \quad & P_{5} \\
  \text{(5)} \quad & P_{23} \\
  \text{(6)} \quad & P_{31} \\
  \text{(7)} \quad & P_{33} \\
  \text{(8)} \quad & (P_{31} \land P_{33}) \rightarrow P_{30} \\
  \text{(9)} \quad & P_{31} \rightarrow \lnot P_{28} \\
  \text{(10)} \quad & P_{31} \rightarrow \lnot P_{32} \\
  \text{(11)} \quad & P_{30} \rightarrow \lnot P_{26} \\
  \vdash \quad & P_{25} \land P_{26} \land P_{27}
\end{align*}
\emph{No premise here rests on scripture alone.}
\paragraph*{Variants}
\leavevmode\par
Each removes named premises from \texttt{reformed} and keeps the rest. The Pauline strand has two lexical premises, so it has two reduced forms.

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{reformed\allowbreak{}Without\allowbreak{}Works\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}Law}} --- Reformed, minus the ἔργα νόμου premise\par\nobreak\noindent

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{reformed\allowbreak{}Without\allowbreak{}Sozo}} --- Reformed, minus the dominical lexical premise\par\nobreak\noindent
The Reformed package without the dominical lexical premise.

\begin{align*}
  \text{(1)} \quad & P_{8} \\

  \text{(16)} \quad & (P_{2} \land P_{3} \land P_{8} \land P_{7} \land P_{6}) \rightarrow P_{24} \\

\end{align*}

\subsection*{Arguments.SolaFide.Results — what does and does not follow}
Entailments are established with \texttt{establish}; refutations name a countermodel — the rival's own reading, written down as a valuation — and use \texttt{refute\_\allowbreak{}with}.

\texttt{lexical\_\allowbreak{}premises\_\allowbreak{}jointly\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing} is the result the encoding exists to make possible: no strand's disputed premise carries the argument, and only removing one from every strand defeats it. \texttt{apostolic\_\allowbreak{}strand\_\allowbreak{}survives\_\allowbreak{}paul\_\allowbreak{}and\_\allowbreak{}luke} records what the third strand changed: the two disputes that used to be jointly decisive are no longer enough.

\paragraph*{Results}
\leavevmode\par
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{reformed\_\allowbreak{}establishes}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Given the Reformed premises, the conclusion follows.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem reformed_establishes : Establishes reformed
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{new\allowbreak{}Perspective\_\allowbreak{}establishes}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The New Perspective establishes it too.} Dunn and Wright reject the traditional reading of ἔργα νόμου; they do not reject justification by faith. Once Jesus' words at Luke 7:50 are in view, denying the Pauline lexical premise no longer blocks the conclusion.

Winning the ἔργα νόμου argument outright is therefore not a defeat of sola fide, and this library says so in a form either side can check. The critics of the New Perspective dispute its premise, not this result.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem newPerspective_establishes : Establishes newPerspective
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{tridentine\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The Tridentine reading, as a valuation: works merit an increase of justification, so salvation is not apart from works; and justification renews the inward man, so it is not forensic only.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def tridentineReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.salvationNotByWorks => False
    | Claim.justificationIsForensicOnly => False
    | Claim.justificationDistinctFromSanctification => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{tridentine\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}establishes}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The Tridentine premises do not establish the conclusion — they entail the negation of its second part, that salvation is not by works.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem tridentine_not_establishes : ¬Establishes tridentine
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{works\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}Law\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The ἔργα νόμου premise is \textbf{not} load-bearing on its own: strip it and the other strands still carry the argument.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem worksOfLaw_not_load_bearing : Establishes reformedWithoutWorksOfLaw
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{sozo\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Nor is the dominical lexical premise: strip it and the other strands still carry the argument.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem sozo_not_load_bearing : Establishes reformedWithoutSozo
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{apostolic\_\allowbreak{}strand\_\allowbreak{}survives\_\allowbreak{}paul\_\allowbreak{}and\_\allowbreak{}luke}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The two disputes that used to decide the argument no longer do.} Deny both the ἔργα νόμου premise and the dominical premise — the pair whose joint removal defeated sola fide before Acts 15 was encoded — and the conclusion still follows, by Peter's speech at Jerusalem.

This is the change the third strand makes, stated as a result rather than left to be inferred from the absence of an old one.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem apostolic_strand_survives_paul_and_luke : Establishes
    reformedWithoutWorksOfLawOrSozo
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{pistis\allowbreak{}Christou\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The objective genitive is not load-bearing for sola fide as a whole. Grant Hays that πίστις Χριστοῦ is Christ's own faithfulness, keep everything else, and the conclusion still follows.

The genitive is the hinge of the Pauline strand, as \texttt{pistis\allowbreak{}Christou\_\allowbreak{}jointly\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing} shows. It is not the hinge of the argument. A reader who wins the genitive for Hays has cost the Reformed case its Pauline route, and not its conclusion.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem pistisChristou_not_load_bearing : Establishes subjectiveGenitive
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{acts15\allowbreak{}Yoke\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{Nor is the yoke.} Grant Jervell that the yoke of Acts 15:10 is Israel's law laid on gentiles, not the law as a condition of salvation, keep everything else, and the conclusion still follows by Paul and by Luke 7:50.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem acts15Yoke_not_load_bearing : Establishes lawObservantLuke
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{neither\allowbreak{}Lexical\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
A reading on which no strand's disputed premise holds: Paul's phrase is about boundary markers, Jesus' σέσωκέν σε is about healing, and Peter's yoke is Israel's law for gentiles.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def neitherLexicalReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.worksOfLawMeansWorksGenerally => False
    | Claim.sozoIsSoteriological => False
    | Claim.acts15YokeIsLawAsCondition => False
    | Claim.justificationByFaithAlone => False
    | Claim.salvationThroughFaith => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{lexical\_\allowbreak{}premises\_\allowbreak{}jointly\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The result worth having.} No strand's disputed premise carries the argument alone, but together they do: remove the ἔργα νόμου premise from Paul, σῴζω from Luke and the yoke from Acts, and sola fide no longer follows, with everything else retained.

So the Reformation's material principle, as encoded here, does not hang on the sense of Paul's ἔργα νόμου. It hangs on that, \emph{or} on the sense of Jesus' σέσωκέν σε, \emph{or} on Peter's yoke — and an opponent must defeat all three. The same holds with the πίστις Χριστοῦ premise in place of ἔργα νόμου; see \texttt{pistis\allowbreak{}Christou\_\allowbreak{}jointly\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing}.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem lexical_premises_jointly_load_bearing : ¬Establishes
    reformedWithoutEveryStrandsPremise
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{neither\allowbreak{}Pistis\allowbreak{}Nor\allowbreak{}Sozo\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
A reading on which Galatians 2:16 names Christ's faithfulness rather than faith in Christ, Jesus' σέσωκέν σε is about healing, and Peter's yoke is Israel's law for gentiles.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def neitherPistisNorSozoReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.pistisChristouObjective => False
    | Claim.sozoIsSoteriological => False
    | Claim.acts15YokeIsLawAsCondition => False
    | Claim.justificationByFaithAlone => False
    | Claim.salvationThroughFaith => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{pistis\allowbreak{}Christou\_\allowbreak{}jointly\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{Within the Pauline strand, faith in Christ is load-bearing.} Deny the objective genitive, and the disputed premise of each other strand, keep the ἔργα νόμου premise and the reading of Galatians as a polemic against circumcision, and sola fide no longer follows.

Excluding works is not enough to reach \emph{faith alone}. Galatians 2:16 must also name the believer's faith as the means, and on the subjective genitive it does not. So the Pauline strand rests on two disputed readings of one verse, either of which costs it the conclusion.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem pistisChristou_jointly_load_bearing : ¬Establishes
    reformedWithoutPistisChristouSozoOrYoke
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{authorship\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{Authorship is not load-bearing.} Grant every critical conclusion — that Ephesians and Titus are not by Paul, and 1 and 2 Peter not by Peter — and the conclusion still follows.

The case reads these letters as canonical scripture rather than as an apostle's testimony, so their authorship is no premise of it — which is why this is proved by weakening (\texttt{entails\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}subset}) from \texttt{reformed\_\allowbreak{}establishes}: the premises added are ones no step reads. What that costs is stated in \texttt{critical\allowbreak{}Authorship}: the letters cannot then serve as evidence of what \emph{Paul} meant, and the ἔργα νόμου premise rests on Romans instead.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem authorship_not_load_bearing : Establishes criticalAuthorship
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{apocalyptic\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The apocalyptic reading, as a valuation: πίστις Χριστοῦ is Christ's faithfulness, δικαιοσύνη θεοῦ is God's deliverance, and faith is not the condition of justification. Everything else it grants.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def apocalypticReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.pistisChristouObjective => False
    | Claim.justificationByFaithAlone => False
    | Claim.salvationThroughFaith => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{apocalyptic\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}establishes}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The apocalyptic reading does not establish sola fide.} It denies the premise the conclusion's third part depends on — that faith is the condition of justification — and so does not reach "through faith".

What it keeps is stated as its own result, \texttt{apocalyptic\_\allowbreak{}establishes\_\allowbreak{}grace\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}works}.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem apocalyptic_not_establishes : ¬Establishes apocalyptic
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{apocalyptic\_\allowbreak{}establishes\_\allowbreak{}grace\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}works}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{What the apocalyptic reading keeps: grace, and not by works.} If δικαιοσύνη θεοῦ is God's act of deliverance, salvation is sheer gift, conditioned on nothing a person does. The reading establishes the first two parts of the conclusion by its own route, and denies the third.

So the dispute between the apocalyptic and Reformed readings is located exactly: it is over \emph{through faith}, and not over grace or works.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem apocalyptic_establishes_grace_not_works : Establishes
    apocalypticOnGraceAndWorks
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{grace\_\allowbreak{}and\_\allowbreak{}works\_\allowbreak{}rest\_\allowbreak{}on\_\allowbreak{}no\_\allowbreak{}lexical\_\allowbreak{}premise}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{Grace, and not by works, rest on no lexical premise.} Remove every one — ἔργα νόμου and πίστις Χριστοῦ from Paul, σῴζω from Luke, the yoke from Acts — and the first two parts of the conclusion still follow, from the texts that say "not of works" in terms and the answer to James.

The lexical disputes decide \emph{through faith alone}: whether the texts that exclude works also name faith as the means. They do not decide whether works are excluded.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem grace_and_works_rest_on_no_lexical_premise : Establishes
    graceAndWorksWithoutAnyLexicalPremise
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{forensic\_\allowbreak{}justification\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{Forensic justification is not load-bearing.} Grant the Finnish reading of Luther that justification is not a declaration only — Christ himself is present in faith — and sola fide still follows. Proved by weakening, like \texttt{authorship\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing}: no step reads the forensic premise.

On what justification \emph{is}, Mannermaa sides with Trent against the Reformed account, and on sola fide with the Reformed against Trent. This result and \texttt{tridentine\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}establishes} together show that denying the forensic account does not by itself deny sola fide. What Trent's denial rests on is narrower: that justification includes the sanctification that grows through works (\texttt{trent\_\allowbreak{}objection\_\allowbreak{}rests\_\allowbreak{}on\_\allowbreak{}its\_\allowbreak{}definition}).

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem forensic_justification_not_load_bearing : Establishes finnish
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{critics\_\allowbreak{}carry\_\allowbreak{}the\_\allowbreak{}pauline\_\allowbreak{}strand}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{The critics carry the Pauline strand.} Deny covenantal nomism with Gathercole, keep the reading of Galatians as a polemic, leave Luke and Acts out, and sola fide follows from Paul alone — with the ἔργα νόμου premise derived rather than assumed.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem critics_carry_the_pauline_strand : Establishes
    paulineStrandOnTheCritics
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{sanders\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Sanders' world, with only Paul to go on: covenantal nomism, so the ἔργα νόμου premise fails and Paul does not reach faith alone.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def sandersReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.worksOfLawMeansWorksGenerally => False
    | Claim.sozoIsSoteriological => False
    | Claim.acts15YokeIsLawAsCondition => False
    | Claim.justificationByFaithAlone => False
    | Claim.salvationThroughFaith => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{sanders\_\allowbreak{}costs\_\allowbreak{}the\_\allowbreak{}pauline\_\allowbreak{}strand}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{And Sanders costs it.} Grant covenantal nomism, and with Dunn's inference the ἔργα νόμου premise is denied; leave Luke and Acts out, and sola fide does not follow from Paul.

Between them, this and \texttt{critics\_\allowbreak{}carry\_\allowbreak{}the\_\allowbreak{}pauline\_\allowbreak{}strand} locate the ἔργα νόμου dispute where the literature has it: in a historical question about Second Temple Judaism, before any question about Paul's Greek.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem sanders_costs_the_pauline_strand : ¬Establishes paulineStrandOnSanders
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{james\allowbreak{}Unharmonised\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
A reading on which James 2:24 stands unharmonised against Paul.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def jamesUnharmonisedReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.james2_24Compatible => False
    | Claim.salvationNotByWorks => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{james\_\allowbreak{}harmonisation\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The argument genuinely depends on answering James. Remove the two premises that harmonise James 2:24 with Paul and sola fide no longer follows, however much of the rest is retained.

An argument for sola fide that does not engage James 2:24 is not merely impolite; it is invalid.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem james_harmonisation_is_load_bearing : ¬Establishes
    reformedWithoutJamesHarmonisation
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{grace\_\allowbreak{}needs\_\allowbreak{}no\_\allowbreak{}answer\_\allowbreak{}to\_\allowbreak{}james}}\par\nobreak\noindent
\textbf{But grace does not need it.} Remove the answer to James and salvation by grace still follows, from Ephesians 2:8, Romans 4:4–5 and Titus 3:5 alone. James 2:24 is load-bearing for \emph{not by works}, the part it appears to contradict, and for nothing else — which the single closing step this argument once had could not show.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem grace_needs_no_answer_to_james : Establishes
    graceWithoutJamesHarmonisation
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\paragraph*{Satisfiability}
\leavevmode\par
\texttt{Entails} is vacuously true over a premise set with no model, so a package built from contradictory premises would establish its conclusion and every gate would pass. Only packages carrying a positive \texttt{Establishes} result need checking: one with a \texttt{¬ Establishes} result is satisfiable already, because its countermodel satisfies every premise.

Not tagged \texttt{@[headline]} — these are claims about the health of the encoding rather than about justification.

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{everything\allowbreak{}Holds\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The reading on which every claim in this argument holds at once: the witness that the packages built from positive grounds are coherent.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def everythingHoldsReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{reformed\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The Reformed package has a model, so \texttt{reformed\_\allowbreak{}establishes} is not vacuous.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem reformed_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable reformed.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{new\allowbreak{}Perspective\allowbreak{}Own\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The New Perspective's own world: Paul's ἔργα νόμου denotes the covenant boundary markers rather than works in general, and justification is by faith regardless. The all-holds reading cannot serve here — this package \emph{denies} a premise, which is the whole point of it.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def newPerspectiveOwnReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.worksOfLawMeansWorksGenerally => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{new\allowbreak{}Perspective\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The New Perspective package has a model.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem newPerspective_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable newPerspective.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{subjective\allowbreak{}Genitive\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Hays's own world: πίστις Χριστοῦ is Christ's faithfulness, and everything else in the Reformed case holds.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def subjectiveGenitiveReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.pistisChristouObjective => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{subjective\allowbreak{}Genitive\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The subjective-genitive package has a model, so \texttt{pistis\allowbreak{}Christou\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing} is not vacuous.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem subjectiveGenitive_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable
    subjectiveGenitive.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{law\allowbreak{}Observant\allowbreak{}Luke\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Jervell's world: the yoke is Israel's law for gentiles, and everything else in the Reformed case holds.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def lawObservantLukeReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.acts15YokeIsLawAsCondition => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{law\allowbreak{}Observant\allowbreak{}Luke\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The law-observant package has a model, so \texttt{acts15\allowbreak{}Yoke\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing} is not vacuous.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem lawObservantLuke_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable
    lawObservantLuke.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{critical\allowbreak{}Authorship\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The critics' world: none of the four disputed letters is by the apostle whose name it bears, and everything else holds.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def criticalAuthorshipReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.ephesiansIsPauline => False
    | Claim.titusIsPauline => False
    | Claim.firstPeterIsPetrine => False
    | Claim.secondPeterIsPetrine => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{critical\allowbreak{}Authorship\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The critical-authorship package has a model, so \texttt{authorship\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing} is not vacuous.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem criticalAuthorship_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable
    criticalAuthorship.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{reformed\allowbreak{}Without\allowbreak{}Works\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}Law\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The Reformed package minus the ἔργα νόμου premise has a model, so \texttt{works\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}Law\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing} is not vacuous. A load-bearing result that held only because its premises could not all be true would be exactly backwards.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem reformedWithoutWorksOfLaw_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable
    reformedWithoutWorksOfLaw.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{reformed\allowbreak{}Without\allowbreak{}Sozo\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
And the same minus the dominical lexical premise.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem reformedWithoutSozo_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable
    reformedWithoutSozo.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{reformed\allowbreak{}Without\allowbreak{}Works\allowbreak{}Of\allowbreak{}Law\allowbreak{}Or\allowbreak{}Sozo\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
And the same minus both, so \texttt{apostolic\_\allowbreak{}strand\_\allowbreak{}survives\_\allowbreak{}paul\_\allowbreak{}and\_\allowbreak{}luke} is not vacuous.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem reformedWithoutWorksOfLawOrSozo_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable
    reformedWithoutWorksOfLawOrSozo.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{apocalyptic\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}Grace\allowbreak{}And\allowbreak{}Works\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The apocalyptic package, asked about grace and works, has a model.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem apocalypticOnGraceAndWorks_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable
    apocalypticOnGraceAndWorks.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{grace\allowbreak{}And\allowbreak{}Works\allowbreak{}Without\allowbreak{}Any\allowbreak{}Lexical\allowbreak{}Premise\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
With every lexical premise removed, the package still has a model.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem graceAndWorksWithoutAnyLexicalPremise_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable
    graceAndWorksWithoutAnyLexicalPremise.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{finnish\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Mannermaa's world: justification is not forensic only, and everything else holds.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def finnishReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.justificationIsForensicOnly => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{finnish\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The Finnish package has a model, so \texttt{forensic\_\allowbreak{}justification\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}load\_\allowbreak{}bearing} is not vacuous.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem finnish_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable finnish.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{critics\allowbreak{}Reading}}\par\nobreak\noindent
Gathercole's world: Second Temple Judaism was not uniformly covenantal nomism, and everything else holds.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
def criticsReading : Valuation Claim :=
  fun a =>
    match a with
    | Claim.secondTempleCovenantalNomism => False
    | x => True
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{pauline\allowbreak{}Strand\allowbreak{}On\allowbreak{}The\allowbreak{}Critics\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
The critics' Pauline package has a model, so \texttt{critics\_\allowbreak{}carry\_\allowbreak{}the\_\allowbreak{}pauline\_\allowbreak{}strand} is not vacuous.

{\small\begin{verbatim}

    paulineStrandOnTheCritics.premises
-- axioms: propext, Quot.sound
\end{verbatim}}
\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{\texttt{grace\allowbreak{}Without\allowbreak{}James\allowbreak{}Harmonisation\_\allowbreak{}is\_\allowbreak{}satisfiable}}\par\nobreak\noindent
With the answer to James removed, the package asked about grace still has a model, so \texttt{grace\_\allowbreak{}needs\_\allowbreak{}no\_\allowbreak{}answer\_\allowbreak{}to\_\allowbreak{}james} is not vacuous.

{\small\begin{verbatim}
theorem graceWithoutJamesHarmonisation_is_satisfiable : Satisfiable
    graceWithoutJamesHarmonisation.premises
